“Zee,” said my host mildly, “you belong to the College of Sages, and ought to be wiser than I am; but, as chief of the Light-preserving Council, it is my duty to take nothing for granted till it is proved to the evidence of my own senses.” Then, turning to me, he asked me several questions about the surface of the earth and the heavenly bodies; upon which, though I answered him to the best of my knowledge, my answers seemed not to satisfy nor convince him. He shook his head quietly, and, changing the subject rather abruptly, asked how I had come down from what he was pleased to call one world to the other. I answered, that under the surface of the earth there were mines containing minerals, or metals, essential to our wants and our progress in all arts and industries; and I then briefly explained the manner in which, while exploring one of those mines, I and my ill-fated friend had obtained a glimpse of the regions into which we had descended, and how the descent had cost him his life; appealing to the rope and grappling-hooks that the child had brought to the house in which I had been at first received, as a witness of the truthfulness of my story.

My host then proceeded to question me as to the habits and modes of life among the races on the upper earth, more especially among those considered to be the most advanced in that civilisation which he was pleased to define “the art of diffusing throughout a community the tranquil happiness which belongs to a virtuous and well-ordered household.” Naturally desiring to represent in the most favourable colours the world from which I came, I touched but slightly, though indulgently, on the antiquated and decaying institutions of Europe, in order to expatiate on the present grandeur and prospective preeminence of that glorious American Republic, in which Europe enviously seeks its model and tremblingly foresees its doom. Selecting for an example of the social life of the United States that city in which progress advances at the fastest rate, I indulged in an animated description of the moral habits of New York. Mortified to see, by the faces of my listeners, that I did not make the favourable impression I had anticipated, I elevated my theme; dwelling on the excellence of democratic institutions, their promotion of tranquil happiness by the government of party, and the mode in which they diffused such happiness throughout the community by preferring, for the exercise of power and the acquisition of honours, the lowliest citizens in point of property, education, and character. Fortunately recollecting the peroration of a speech, on the purifying influences of American democracy and their destined spread over the world, made by a certain eloquent senator (for whose vote in the Senate a Railway Company, to which my two brothers belonged, had just paid 20,000 dollars), I wound up by repeating its glowing predictions of the magnificent future that smiled upon mankind﻿—when the flag of freedom should float over an entire continent, and two hundred millions of intelligent citizens, accustomed from infancy to the daily use of revolvers, should apply to a cowering universe the doctrine of the Patriot Monroe.

When I had concluded, my host gently shook his head, and fell into a musing study, making a sign to me and his daughter to remain silent while he reflected. And after a time he said, in a very earnest and solemn tone, “If you think as you say, that you, though a stranger, have received kindness at the hands of me and mine, I adjure you to reveal nothing to any other of our people respecting the world from which you came, unless, on consideration, I give you permission to do so. Do you consent to this request?”

“Of course I pledge my word to it,” said I, somewhat amazed; and I extended my right hand to grasp his. But he placed my hand gently on his forehead and his own right hand on my breast, which is the custom amongst this race in all matters of promise or verbal obligations. Then turning to his daughter, he said, “And you, Zee, will not repeat to anyone what the stranger has said, or may say, to me or to you, of a world other than our own.” Zee rose and kissed her father on the temples, saying, with a smile, “A *Gy*’s tongue is wanton, but love can fetter it fast. And if, my father, you fear lest a chance word from me or yourself could expose our community to danger, by a desire to explore a world beyond us, will not a wave of the *vril*, properly impelled, wash even the memory of what we have heard the stranger say out of the tablets of the brain?”

“What is the *vril*?” I asked.

Therewith Zee began to enter into an explanation of which I understood very little, for there is no word in any language I know which is an exact synonym for *vril*. I should call it electricity, except that it comprehends in its manifold branches other forces of nature, to which, in our scientific nomenclature, differing names are assigned, such as magnetism, galvanism, etc. These people consider that in *vril* they have arrived at the unity in natural energetic agencies, which has been conjectured by many philosophers above ground, and which Faraday thus intimates under the more cautious term of correlation:﻿—

“I have long held an opinion,” says that illustrious experimentalist, “almost amounting to a conviction, in common, I believe, with many other lovers of natural knowledge, that the various forms under which the forces of matter are made manifest, have one common origin; or, in other words, are so directly related and mutually dependent, that they are convertible, as it were, into one another, and possess equivalents of power in their action.”

These subterranean philosophers assert that by one operation of *vril*, which Faraday would perhaps call “atmospheric magnetism,” they can influence the variations of temperature﻿—in plain words, the weather; that by other operations, akin to those ascribed to mesmerism, electro-biology, odic force, etc., but applied scientifically through *vril* conductors, they can exercise influence over minds, and bodies animal and vegetable, to an extent not surpassed in the romances of our mystics. To all such agencies they give the common name of *vril*. Zee asked me if, in my world, it was not known that all the faculties of the mind could be quickened to a degree unknown in the waking state, by trance or vision, in which the thoughts of one brain could be transmitted to another, and knowledge be thus rapidly interchanged. I replied, that there were amongst us stories told of such trance or vision, and that I had heard much and seen something of the mode in which they were artificially effected, as in mesmeric clairvoyance; but that these practices had fallen much into disuse or contempt, partly because of the gross impostures to which they had been made subservient, and partly because, even where the effects upon certain abnormal constitutions were genuinely produced, the effects, when fairly examined and analysed, were very unsatisfactory﻿—not to be relied upon for any systematic truthfulness or any practical purpose, and rendered very mischievous to credulous persons by the superstitions they tended to produce. Zee received my answers with much benignant attention, and said that similar instances of abuse and credulity had been familiar to their own scientific experience in the infancy of their knowledge, and while the properties of *vril* were misapprehended, but that she reserved further discussion on this subject till I was more fitted to enter into it. She contented herself with adding, that it was through the agency of *vril*, while I had been placed in the state of trance, that I had been made acquainted with the rudiments of their language; and that she and her father, who, alone of the family, took the pains to watch the experiment, had acquired a greater proportionate knowledge of my language than I of their own; partly because my language was much simpler than theirs, comprising far less of complex ideas; and partly because their organisation was, by hereditary culture, much more ductile and more readily capable of acquiring knowledge than mine. At this I secretly demurred; and having had, in the course of a practical life, to sharpen my wits, whether at home or in travel, I could not allow that my cerebral organisation could possibly be duller than that of people who had lived all their lives by lamplight. However, while I was thus thinking, Zee quietly pointed her forefinger at my forehead and sent me to sleep.

===Chinese===
在这座宏大的宅邸里，分派给了我一间独用的房间。房中布置得雅致而富于奇趣，却不带那些较公共的厅堂里所炫耀的金属工艺与宝石的华彩。墙上悬挂着以植物茎秆和纤维织成的杂色席垫，地面铺的也是同一质料。

床不带帷幔，铁制的床架安放在水晶球上；铺盖是一种类似棉布的白色薄物。室内有数排搁架，架上置书。一道挂帘的凹室通向一间满是鸣禽的鸟舍；舍中的鸟，除了一种美丽的鸽类之外，我不认得有一只与我曾在世上见过的相似，而那鸽又与我们的鸽子有别，头顶耸着一冠蓝青色的羽毛。这些鸟全都受过训练，能唱精巧的曲调，其技艺远胜我们那些会吹哨的红腹灰雀——后者难得学会两支以上的曲子，而且据我所信，也不能把那几支曲子齐声合唱。听着我鸟舍中的这些歌喉，人简直会以为自己置身于一家歌剧院。那里有二重唱与三重唱，有四重唱与合唱，全都编排得如同一部完整的乐曲。我想让鸟儿噤声么？只消拉过一道帘子罩住鸟舍，鸟儿发觉自己被撇在黑暗里，歌声便悄然止息。另一处开口则构成一扇窗，窗上不装玻璃，但一触弹簧，便有一扇遮板自地面升起，其质料不及玻璃透明，却仍足够澄澈，可使外面的景致以柔和的面目透入眼帘。窗上连着一座露台——毋宁说是一座悬空的花园——园中长着许多姿态曼妙的植物和光彩灼灼的花卉。这间屋子及其附属陈设，细部固然奇异，就整体而言却仍不悖于现代的奢华观念；倘若发现它们连在英国某位公爵夫人或某位当红法国作家的居室上，也必会引人叹赏。在我到来之前，这是琪的卧房；她好客地把它让给了我。

在我上一章所述的那番苏醒之后几小时，我正独自躺在卧榻上，竭力把心思凝定于揣想——揣想我身投其中的这个民族的性情与族类——这时，主人和他的女儿琪走进了房间。主人仍旧说着我的母语，十分客气地探问，与我交谈是否于我为快，抑或我更愿独处。我回答说，承蒙赐予我这样一个机会，得以表达我对所受款待与礼遇的谢忱——在这个于我全然陌生的国度里——并得以习得足够的习俗礼仪，庶几不因无知而开罪于人，我实觉荣宠且感激。

说话时，我自然已从卧榻上起身；可是琪竟简截地命我重新躺下，令我颇为窘迫；她的声音与眼色尽管温和，其中却自有一种使我不能不服从的力量。于是她坦然自若地在床脚坐下，她的父亲则在数步之外的长榻上就座。

“可你究竟是从世上哪一方来的，”主人问道，“竟至于我们于你显得这般奇异，你于我们亦然？凡与我族不同的诸种族，其单独的标本我几乎都已见过，唯有那些原始的蛮人除外——他们栖居在未经垦辟的自然中最荒凉、最僻远的角落，除从火山之火得来的光以外不知别样的光，并且甘愿像许多爬行的、蠕动的、乃至飞翔的东西一样，在黑暗中摸索自己的路。不过你当然不会是那些野蛮部落的一员；而另一方面，你似乎也不属于任何文明的民族。”

这末后一句评语使我微觉刺恼，便回答说，我有幸属于世上最文明的民族之一；至于光，我固然钦佩我的主人及其同胞以何等的巧思、何等的不惜工本，设法照亮了太阳光线所不能穿透的那些地域，然而我终究不能理解：凡是亲眼见过天上诸光体的人，怎能把出于人类必需而发明的人造灯火，拿来同天光的辉耀相较。但主人说，凡与他族类不同的种族，他大抵见过标本，唯有他方才提及的那班可悲的蛮人除外。那么，莫非他从未踏上过地球的表面，抑或他所指的，不过是埋在大地脏腑之间的那些社会？

主人默然有顷；他的面容上露出几分惊异——这个种族的人在任何境况之下，无论何等异乎寻常，都极少流露这种神情。但琪更为聪敏，脱口说道：“父亲，您看，古老的传说之中自有真实；凡是历代为一切部族共同信奉的传说，总含有真实。”

“琪，”主人和婉地说，“你隶属贤哲学院，理应比我有智慧；但我身为护光议事会之首，职责所在，凡未经我自己感官的证据证实的事，一概不得率然视为当然。”随后他转向我，就地球的表面与天体问了我几个问题；我虽竭尽所知一一作答，我的回答似乎既未使他满意，也未将他说服。他静静地摇了摇头，随即颇为突兀地转过话题，问我如何从他所乐于称谓的一个世界降到了另一个世界。我答道，地表之下有矿，矿中蕴藏着矿物，或者说金属，于我们的需要、于我们一切技艺与产业的进步都不可或缺；接着我约略说明，我和我那位罹祸的朋友在勘探其中一矿之时，如何得以窥见我们下降所至的那片地界，那次下降又如何使他丧了性命；我又援引那孩子带到我先初受接待的那座宅子里来的绳索与抓钩，作为我所言属实的一个见证。

于是主人进而问我在上界诸种族中间的习性与生活方式，尤其是那些被认为在最先进的文明中居前的种族——那种文明，他乐于下一个界说，谓之“将属于一个贤良而井然有序之家的恬静幸福播散于全社会的艺术”。我自然愿意把我所来自的世界描画得尽量光彩，于是对欧洲那些陈旧而衰朽的制度只轻轻带过——虽是带着宽宥——以便纵笔铺陈那光荣的美利坚共和国当今的宏伟与前程的超群绝伦：欧洲正妒羡地以它为楷模，又战栗地从中预睹自己的劫数。我选取合众国社会生活的一个例证——那座进步以最快速率推进的城市——兴味盎然地描摹了一番纽约的道德风习。及至从两位听者的面色看出，我并未造成自己原先期待的良佳印象，心中不免怏怏，于是把题目再抬高一格：细论民主制度的优越，它们如何以政党的治理促进恬静的幸福，又如何把这种幸福传布于全社会——其法是在行使权力与擢授荣衔之际，专择财产、教育、品性诸方面最卑微的公民。幸而我记起一位雄辩的参议员一场演说的结语（我两个兄弟所在的一家铁路公司，刚为他在参议院中的那一票付了 20,000 美元），那演说讲的正是美国民主的净化之力及其注定布满全球的命运；我便援引其中灼灼生辉的预言作结：人类面前微笑着一幅壮丽的远景——到自由的旗帜飘扬于一整个大陆之上之时，到两亿有智的公民——自幼惯于日日使用左轮手枪——把爱国者门罗的学说施用于一个瑟瑟发抖的宇宙之时。

我说完之后，主人轻轻摇了摇头，陷入沉思，并示意我与他女儿保持缄默，容他思量。过了一会儿，他以极为恳切而庄重的声调说道：“倘若你如你所言，认为你虽是外人，却在我和我一家手中受了善意，那么我恳求你，关于你所来自的那个世界，除经我审虑之后给予许可外，切勿向我们族中的任何他人透露半句。你可应允这一请求？”

“那是自然，我以此担保。”我说，心中颇感诧异；说着我伸出右手去握他的手。但他把我的手轻轻按在他的额头，又将他自己的右手按在我的胸前——这是此族在一切许诺或口头义务之事上的惯例。然后他转向女儿，说道：“还有你，琪，这位客人对我或对你已经说过的、或将要说出的、关于我们这个世界以外的任何话，你都不得向任何人复述。”琪起身，吻了吻父亲的双鬓，微笑道：“*姬*（Gy）的舌头惯于放肆，但爱能将它牢牢锁住。再者，父亲，倘若您担心我或您偶然脱口的一语，会激起他人探求我们以外之世界的欲念，从而置我们的社会于危险，那么，只要推动得当，一阵*弗里尔*（Vril）的波澜，岂不能连我们听这位客人所说的话的记忆，也从头脑的书板上冲洗干净？”

“什么是*弗里尔*？”我问。

于是琪开始了一番解说，而我所能领会的甚少，因为在我所通晓的任何语言里，没有一个词堪作*弗里尔*的严格同义词。我本该称它为电，只是它在繁多的支脉中还包纳着自然界的其他力量——在我们的科学命名法里，那些力量各有相异的名称，如磁力、伽伐尼电流之类。这个民族认为，他们凭*弗里尔*已在自然的诸能量作用中达到了统一；这一统一，地上许多哲学家早有猜测，而法拉第在“相关”（correlation）这一更为审慎的名目下如此暗示道：——

“我久已怀有一种意见，”那位杰出的实验家说道，“几乎达于确信；我相信，这也是许多其他自然知识爱好者的共同意见：物质之力借以显现的各种形式，有一个共同的起源；或者换一句话说，它们彼此直接相联、互相依存，以至于可以说能够相互转化，并在其作用中各具力量的当量。”

这些地下的哲人断言：凭*弗里尔*的一种作用——法拉第或许会称之为“大气磁性”——他们能影响温度的变化，用浅白的话说，就是天气；凭另一些作用——与归于催眠术、电生物学、奥德力等等名下的那些作用相类、却是经由*弗里尔*导体以科学之法施用的——他们能对心灵、对动物与植物的身体施展影响，其所及之深远，连我们神秘主义者的传奇也不曾超越。对这一切作用之力，他们同以*弗里尔*一名名之。琪问我，在我那个世界里，人们是否也知道：心灵的一切官能，可借迷睡或异象振奋到清醒状态中未见的程度——在那种状态里，一个头脑的思想能传送到另一个头脑，知识便由此迅速互通。我回答说，我们中间确有讲述这类迷睡或异象的传闻；我也听得很多，并亲眼见过一点它们如何被人为造成的方式，例如催眠透视之类；但这些做法已大半废弛或为人所轻，一部分是因为它们曾被驱作粗劣骗局的工具，一部分是因为，即便在某些反常的体质上确实造成了效应，那些效应一经公正的检视与分析，也极不能令人满意——于任何系统性的真实或任何实用的目的都无从信赖，还因它们易于滋生的迷信，给轻信之徒带来很大的祸害。琪以极慈和、极专注的态度听取了我的回答，说类似的滥用与轻信之事，在他们自己知识初开的年代、在*弗里尔*的特性尚遭误解之时，也是他们的科学经验中素所习见的；不过这个题目，她姑且保留，待我更相宜领受时再谈。她只满足于补充一句：先前令我得谙他们语言的初步，正是凭*弗里尔*之力、当我被置于迷睡状态时所成就；而她与她的父亲——全家唯有他们二人不惮烦劳、守着那场实验——于我的语言所获的相称知识，比我对他们语言的所获为多；一部分是因为我的语言远比他们的简单，所含的复杂观念少得多；一部分是因为他们的机体组织，经世世代代的陶冶，远比我的柔韧，更易于汲取知识。这话我私下不敢苟同；我一生涉历实务，无论居家还是远游，都须磨砺心智，实在不能承认我的脑部组织竟可能比一群一辈子借着灯光过活的人迟钝。然而，正当我如此转念之际，琪悄悄将食指朝我的额头一点，便把我送入了睡眠。

## VIII / 八

===Original===
When I once more awoke I saw by my bedside the child who had brought the rope and grappling-hooks to the house in which I had been first received, and which, as I afterwards learned, was the residence of the chief magistrate of the tribe. The child, whose name was Taë (pronounced *Tar-ēē*), was the magistrate’s eldest son. I found that during my last sleep or trance I had made still greater advance in the language of the country, and could converse with comparative ease and fluency.

This child was singularly handsome, even for the beautiful race to which he belonged, with a countenance very manly in aspect for his years, and with a more vivacious and energetic expression than I had hitherto seen in the serene and passionless faces of the men. He brought me the tablet on which I had drawn the mode of my descent, and had also sketched the head of the horrible reptile that had scared me from my friend’s corpse. Pointing to that part of the drawing, Taë put to me a few questions respecting the size and form of the monster, and the cave or chasm from which it had emerged. His interest in my answers seemed so grave as to divert him for a while from any curiosity as to myself or my antecedents. But to my great embarrassment, seeing how I was pledged to my host, he was just beginning to ask me where I came from, when Zee fortunately entered, and, overhearing him, said, “Taë, give to our guest any information he may desire, but ask none from him in return. To question him who he is, whence he comes, or wherefore he is here, would be a breach of the law which my father has laid down for this house.”

“So be it,” said Taë, pressing his hand to his heart; and from that moment, till the one in which I saw him last, this child, with whom I became very intimate, never once put to me any of the questions thus interdicted.

===Chinese===
当我再度醒来时，看见床边坐着那个孩子——当初把绳索和抓钩送到我最初受到接待的那座宅子里去的，正是他；据我后来得知，那座宅子便是部族首席长官的居所。这孩子名叫塔伊（读音 *Tar-ēē*），是那位长官的长子。我发觉，经过上一场睡眠——或者说迷睡——之后，我对这个国度的语言又取得了更大的进步，已能相当从容流利地与人交谈了。

这孩子生得俊逸出众，即便在他所属的那个美丽种族中也属难得：就其年龄而论，面容已颇具男子气概，而其神情之活泼、之富有生气，更非我历来在此族男子那些安详恬静、不露欲念的面孔上所能见到。他把我那块写字板拿来了，板上画着我下降的方式，还勾勒着那可怕爬虫的头颅——正是它吓得我离开了朋友的尸身。塔伊指着画中的那一处，就怪物的大小与形状，以及它钻出来的那个洞穴或深坑，向我提了几个问题。他对我回答所表现出的兴趣甚是郑重，一时间竟把对我本人和我的来历的好奇都搁在了一旁。然而令我大感窘迫的是——因为我已向主人承担了义务——他正要开口问我从何处来，幸而琪这时走了进来，听见他的问话，便说道：“塔伊，我们的客人想知晓什么，你尽管告诉他，却不要反过来向他探问。若盘问他是何人、从何处来、为何到此，便违背了我父亲为本宅立下的规矩。”

“就这样吧。”塔伊说着，把手按在心口；从那一刻起，直到我最后一眼见到他的时候，这个后来与我甚为亲密的孩子，从未向我提过一个这样被禁止的问题。

## X / 十

===Original===
The word *Ana* (pronounced broadly *Arna*) corresponds with our plural *men*; *An* (pronounced *Arn*), the singular, with *man*. The word for woman is *Gy* (pronounced hard, as in *Guy*); it forms itself into *Gy-ei* for the plural, but the *G* becomes soft in the plural like *Jy-ei*. They have a proverb to the effect that this difference in pronunciation is symbolical, for that the female sex is soft in the concrete, but hard to deal with in the individual. The *Gy-ei* are in the fullest enjoyment of all the rights of equality with males, for which certain philosophers above ground contend.

===Chinese===
安-纳（*Ana*）一词（宽读近于 *Arna*），相当于我们的复数“男人们”；其单数安（*An*）（读作 *Arn*），相当于“男人”。女子的称谓是姬（*Gy*）（发硬音，如同 *Guy*）；它变化为姬-埃（*Gy-ei*）即成复数，惟复数中的 G 转为软音，读如 *Jy-ei*。他们有一句谚语，大意说这种读音上的差别具有象征意味：女性就整体而言是柔软的，落到个体身上却难以对付。姬-埃们充分享有与男性平等的一切权利——那正是地上某些哲学家力争的东西。

===Original===
In childhood they perform the offices of work and labour impartially with the boys, and, indeed, in the earlier age appropriated to the destruction of animals irreclaimably hostile, the girls are frequently preferred, as being by constitution more ruthless under the influence of fear or hate. In the interval between infancy and the marriageable age familiar intercourse between the sexes is suspended. At the marriageable age it is renewed, never with worse consequences than those which attend upon marriage. All arts and vocations allotted to the one sex are open to the other, and the *Gy-ei* arrogate to themselves a superiority in all those abstruse and mystical branches of reasoning, for which they say the *Ana* are unfitted by a duller sobriety of understanding, or the routine of their matter-of-fact occupations, just as young ladies in our own world constitute themselves authorities in the subtlest points of theological doctrine, for which few men, actively engaged in worldly business, have sufficient learning or refinement of intellect. Whether owing to early training in gymnastic exercises or to their constitutional organisation, the *Gy-ei* are usually superior to the *Ana* in physical strength (an important element in the consideration and maintenance of female rights). They attain to loftier stature, and amid their rounder proportions are embedded sinews and muscles as hardy as those of the other sex. Indeed they assert that, according to the original laws of nature, females were intended to be larger than males, and maintain this dogma by reference to the earliest formations of life in insects, and in the most ancient family of the vertebrata﻿—viz., fishes﻿—in both of which the females are generally large enough to make a meal of their consorts if they so desire. Above all, the *Gy-ei* have a readier and more concentred power over that mysterious fluid or agency which contains the element of destruction, with a larger portion of that sagacity which comprehends dissimulation. Thus they cannot only defend themselves against all aggressions from the males, but could, at any moment when he least expected his danger, terminate the existence of an offending spouse. To the credit of the *Gy-ei* no instance of their abuse of this awful superiority in the art of destruction is on record for several ages. The last that occurred in the community I speak of appears (according to their chronology) to have been about two thousand years ago. A *Gy*, then, in a fit of jealousy, slew her husband; and this abominable act inspired such terror among the males that they emigrated in a body and left all the *Gy-ei* to themselves. The history runs that the widowed *Gy-ei*, thus reduced to despair, fell upon the murderess when in her sleep (and therefore unarmed), and killed her, and then entered into a solemn obligation amongst themselves to abrogate forever the exercise of their extreme conjugal powers, and to inculcate the same obligation forever and ever on their female children. By this conciliatory process, a deputation despatched to the fugitive consorts succeeded in persuading many to return, but those who did return were mostly the elder ones. The younger, either from too craven a doubt of their consorts, or too high an estimate of their own merits, rejected all overtures, and, remaining in other communities, were caught up there by other mates, with whom perhaps they were no better off. But the loss of so large a portion of the male youth operated as a salutary warning on the *Gy-ei*, and confirmed them in the pious resolution to which they had pledged themselves. Indeed it is now popularly considered that, by long hereditary disuse, the *Gy-ei* have lost both the aggressive and defensive superiority over the *Ana* which they once possessed, just as in the inferior animals above the earth many peculiarities in their original formation, intended by nature for their protection, gradually fade or become inoperative when not needed under altered circumstances. I should be sorry, however, for any *An* who induced a *Gy* to make the experiment whether he or she were the stronger.

===Chinese===
幼年时期，她们与男童一样承担劳作的职分；而且，在那个专用于消灭无可救药之敌兽的早年阶段，女童往往更受偏爱，因为论天性，她们在恐惧或憎恨的影响之下更为狠辣。从幼年到婚龄之间，两性的亲密交往暂告中断；到了婚龄便重新恢复，其后果之坏，从无逾于婚姻本身所伴随者。分派给一种性别的技艺与职业，对另一种性别也一律开放；而姬-埃们自命在一切艰深玄奥的推理学问上高人一等，她们说，安-纳们或因理解力较为迟钝持重、或因实际事务的刻板例行，均不宜问津——恰如我们这个世界的年轻女士们，动辄自居为神学教义中最精微之点的权威，而积极从事世俗事务的男子，罕有具备足够的学识或心智之精细者。无论当归因于体操的早期训练，还是归因于她们的天生体质，姬-埃们在体力上通常胜过安-纳们（这是考虑和维护女性权利的一个重要因素）。她们身量更高，而在较为丰圆的形体之中，蕴嵌着与另一性别同样强健的筋腱与肌肉。她们甚至断言，依照太初的自然法则，雌性本就该比雄性更为高大；并援引昆虫中最早成形的生命形态，以及脊椎动物最古老的家族——即鱼类——来维护这条教义：在这两类生物中，雌性通常都大到足以把她们的配偶当作一餐吃掉，只要她们愿意。尤其是，姬-埃们对那种蕴含毁灭要素的神秘流质或力量，掌握着更为敏捷、更为凝聚的权能，还禀有更大分量的、深谙掩饰之道的机敏。因此，她们不仅能抵御男性的一切侵犯，而且能在丈夫最料不到危险临头的任何时刻，了结一位失礼的配偶的性命。为姬-埃们增光的是，数个世纪以来，未曾有她们滥用这种可畏的毁灭绝技之优势的记载。我所谈及的这个社区中，最后一次此类事件，据他们的纪年推算，大约发生在两千年前。当时一位姬妒火中烧，杀死了自己的丈夫；这一可憎的行径在男性中间激起如此恐怖，以致他们集体出走，把姬-埃们尽数撇下不管。史载那些由此陷入绝望的寡居姬-埃，趁那凶手熟睡之际（因而手无寸铁）扑向她，将她杀死；随后她们彼此缔结了一项庄严的盟约，永远废止她们那极端婚姻权力的行使，并将同一义务世世代代谆谆训诲于她们的女性后代。经此修好之举，派往那些出逃丈夫们那里的使团劝得多人归来；但归来者多为年长者。较年轻的那些，或因对妻子们疑虑太深而过于怯懦，或因对自己优点估价太高，拒绝了所有的劝请，留在别的社区，被当地另外的配偶搭上了钩，而与那些新配偶，他们或许过得也并不更好。但失去了如此大一部分青年男性，对姬-埃们不啻一记有益的警诫，使她们更坚定了自己立誓发愿的那份虔敬决心。的确，如今人们普遍认为，由于世代相传的长期废置，姬-埃们已丧失了她们一度拥有的对安-纳们的攻守优势——恰如地上那些低等动物，其原始构造中许多由自然安排用以护身的特性，在境况改变、不再需要时，便逐渐消退或失其效用。不过，无论哪位安若诱使一位姬来试一试他俩孰强孰弱，我都要为他捏一把汗。

===Original===
From the incident I have narrated, the Ana date certain alterations in the marriage customs, tending, perhaps, somewhat to the advantage of the male. They now bind themselves in wedlock only for three years; at the end of each third year either male or female can divorce the other and is free to marry again. At the end of ten years the *An* has the privilege of taking a second wife, allowing the first to retire if she so please. These regulations are for the most part a dead letter; divorces and polygamy are extremely rare, and the marriage state now seems singularly happy and serene among this astonishing people;﻿—the *Gy-ei*, notwithstanding their boastful superiority in physical strength and intellectual abilities, being much curbed into gentle manners by the dread of separation or of a second wife, and the *Ana* being very much the creatures of custom, and not, except under great aggravation, likely to exchange for hazardous novelties faces and manners to which they are reconciled by habit. But there is one privilege the *Gy-ei* carefully retain, and the desire for which perhaps forms the secret motive of most lady assertors of woman rights above ground. They claim the privilege, here usurped by men, of proclaiming their love and urging their suit; in other words, of being the wooing party rather than the wooed. Such a phenomenon as an old maid does not exist among the *Gy-ei*. Indeed it is very seldom that a *Gy* does not secure any *An* upon whom she sets her heart, if his affections be not strongly engaged elsewhere. However coy, reluctant, and prudish, the male she courts may prove at first, yet her perseverance, her ardour, her persuasive powers, her command over the mystic agencies of *vril*, are pretty sure to run down his neck into what we call “the fatal noose.” Their argument for the reversal of that relationship of the sexes which the blind tyranny of man has established on the surface of the earth, appears cogent, and is advanced with a frankness which might well be commended to impartial consideration. They say, that of the two the female is by nature of a more loving disposition than the male﻿—that love occupies a larger space in her thoughts, and is more essential to her happiness, and that therefore she ought to be the wooing party; that otherwise the male is a shy and dubitant creature﻿—that he has often a selfish predilection for the single state﻿—that he often pretends to misunderstand tender glances and delicate hints﻿—that, in short, he must be resolutely pursued and captured. They add, moreover, that unless the *Gy* can secure the *An* of her choice, and one whom she would not select out of the whole world becomes her mate, she is not only less happy than she otherwise would be, but she is not so good a being, that her qualities of heart are not sufficiently developed; whereas the *An* is a creature that less lastingly concentrates his affections on one object; that if he cannot get the *Gy* whom he prefers he easily reconciles himself to another *Gy*; and, finally, that at the worst, if he is loved and taken care of, it is less necessary to the welfare of his existence that he should love as well as be loved; he grows contented with his creature comforts, and the many occupations of thought which he creates for himself.

===Chinese===
自所述那次事件起，安-纳们便将婚俗上的若干更改纪元于此——这些更改，或许稍稍有利于男方。如今他们缔结婚姻只以三年为期；每满三年，无论男女，任一方都可离弃另一方，自由另嫁另娶。满十年后，安享有再娶一妻的特权，并许原配退位，倘若她愿意。这些规章大抵形同虚设；离婚与重婚极为罕见，婚姻生活在这个惊人的民族中间如今显得格外幸福安宁；——姬-埃们尽管自夸体力与智力上的优越，却因惧怕离异、惧怕再娶之妻，大受约束而变得温婉有礼；而安-纳们又大抵是习惯的造物，除非受到极度的刺激，断不肯拿业已习以为常的面容与举止，去交换吉凶难卜的新奇。但有一项特权，姬-埃们小心保留着；对这项特权的欲望，或许正是地上大多数鼓吹女权的女士们心底的秘密动机。她们要求的这项特权——在地上被男人们僭据着的——便是宣告爱情、展开追求；换言之，是做求婚的一方，而非被求的一方。老处女这种现象，在姬-埃中间并不存在。的确，只要那位安的心不曾另有强系，一位姬凡属心之所属，几乎无有不弄到手的。她所追求的男子起初无论怎样扭捏、怎样推拒、怎样假作端庄，她的坚忍、她的热烈、她的说服之才、她对弗里尔（vril）神秘力量的驾驭，终归十有八九会套上他的脖颈，收紧成我们所谓的“致命绞索”。她们主张颠倒两性间那种由男人的盲目暴政在地上确立的关系，其论据看似有力，提出时又那样坦率，很值得推荐给不偏不倚的考量。她们说，两性之中，雌性天生比雄性更多情——爱情在她的思绪中占据更大的位置，对她的幸福更为攸关，因此她理当做求婚的一方；否则，雄性便是一种羞怯而迟疑不决的造物——他常常出于私心偏爱独身——他常常装作不解温柔的顾盼与精微的暗示——总而言之，必须果断地追逐他、捕获他。她们还说，姬若不能得到她所选中的安，而与一个她从全世界里本不会挑选的人结为配偶，那她便不仅不如原本那样幸福，而且也成不了那样好的人，她心灵的品质便得不到充分的发展；反之，安这种造物，把感情长久专注于一个对象的本领较小；倘若得不到他属意的姬，他很容易便与另一位姬言归于好；而说到底，最不济的情形是，只要有人爱他、照料他，他是否也付出爱，对他生存的福祉便无甚必要；他会心满意足于生活的舒适，满足于他为自己创造出来的种种心智上的消遣。

===Original===
Whatever may be said as to this reasoning, the system works well for the male; for being thus sure that he is truly and ardently loved, and that the more coy and reluctant he shows himself, the more determination to secure him increases, he generally contrives to make his consent dependent on such conditions as he thinks the best calculated to insure, if not a blissful, at least a peaceful life. Each individual *An* has his own hobbies, his own ways, his own predilections, and, whatever they may be, he demands a promise of full and unrestrained concession to them. This, in the pursuit of her object, the *Gy* readily promises; and as the characteristic of this extraordinary people is an implicit veneration for truth, and her word once given is never broken even by the giddiest *Gy*, the conditions stipulated for are religiously observed. In fact, notwithstanding all their abstract rights and powers, the *Gy-ei* are the most amiable, conciliatory, and submissive wives I have ever seen even in the happiest households above ground. It is an aphorism among them, that “where a *Gy* loves it is her pleasure to obey.” It will be observed that in the relationship of the sexes I have spoken only of marriage, for such is the moral perfection to which this community has attained, that any illicit connection is as little possible amongst them as it would be to a couple of linnets during the time they agree to live in pairs.

===Chinese===
这套道理且随人评说，这套制度于男性却运转甚佳；因为既然确知自己被人真心而热烈地爱着，而且自己越是扭捏推拒，对方把他弄到手的决心便越发增长，他便总能设法使自己的应允附带种种条件——他认定那些条件最有助于保障一种纵非极乐、至少安宁的生活。每一位安都有自己的癖好、自己的做派、自己的偏嗜，而无论这些是什么，他都要求对方许诺对其全盘地、毫无拘束地让步。为了追求她的目标，姬总是慨然应允；而这个非凡民族的天性是对真理怀有不容置疑的敬畏，话一出口便永不反悔，连最轻佻的姬也不例外，故而约定的条件无不恪守不渝。事实上，尽管拥有那一切抽象的权利与权能，姬-埃们仍是我所见过的最和蔼、最肯迁就、最柔顺的妻子，即便地上最幸福的家庭也无可比拟。她们中间流传着一句格言：“姬凡有所爱，服从即是她的乐趣。”细心的读者当会注意到，论及两性关系时我只谈了婚姻；因为这个社区在道德上已达于如此完美的境地，任何苟且的结合在他们中间之不可能，恰如一对红雀在双双同居期间之不可能有越轨之举。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
Nothing had more perplexed me in seeking to reconcile my sense to the existence of regions extending below the surface of the earth, and habitable by beings, if dissimilar from, still, in all material points of organism, akin to those in the upper world, than the contradiction thus presented to the doctrine in which, I believe, most geologists and philosophers concur﻿—viz., that though with us the sun is the great source of heat, yet the deeper we go beneath the crust of the earth, the greater is the increasing heat, being, it is said, found in the ratio of a degree for every foot, commencing from fifty feet below the surface. But though the domains of the tribe I speak of were, on the higher ground, so comparatively near to the surface, that I could account for a temperature, therein, suitable to organic life, yet even the ravines and valleys of that realm were much less hot than philosophers would deem possible at such a depth﻿—certainly not warmer than the south of France, or at least of Italy. And according to all the accounts I received, vast tracts immeasurably deeper beneath the surface, and in which one might have thought only salamanders could exist, were inhabited by innumerable races organised like ourselves. I cannot pretend in any way to account for a fact which is so at variance with the recognised laws of science, nor could Zee much help me towards a solution of it. She did but conjecture that sufficient allowance had not been made by our philosophers for the extreme porousness of the interior earth﻿—the vastness of its cavities and irregularities, which served to create free currents of air and frequent winds﻿—and for the various modes in which heat is evaporated and thrown off. She allowed, however, that there was a depth at which the heat was deemed to be intolerable to such organised life as was known to the experience of the Vril-ya, though their philosophers believed that even in such places life of some kind, life sentient, life intellectual, would be found abundant and thriving, could the philosophers penetrate to it. “Wherever the All-Good builds,” said she, “there, be sure, He places inhabitants. He loves not empty dwellings.” She added, however, that many changes in temperature and climate had been effected by the skill of the Vril-ya, and that the agency of *vril* had been successfully employed in such changes. She described a subtle and life-giving medium called *Lai*, which I suspect to be identical with the ethereal oxygen of Dr. Lewins, wherein work all the correlative forces united under the name of *vril*; and contended that wherever this medium could be expanded, as it were, sufficiently for the various agencies of *vril* to have ample play, a temperature congenial to the highest forms of life could be secured. She said also, that it was the belief of their naturalists that flowers and vegetation had been produced originally (whether developed from seeds borne from the surface of the earth in the earlier convulsions of nature, or imported by the tribes that first sought refuge in cavernous hollows) through the operations of the light constantly brought to bear on them, and the gradual improvement in culture. She said also, that since the *vril* light had superseded all other light-giving bodies, the colours of flower and foliage had become more brilliant, and vegetation had acquired larger growth.

Leaving these matters to the consideration of those better competent to deal with them, I must now devote a few pages to the very interesting questions connected with the language of the Vril-ya.

===Chinese===
在力求使自己的理智接受下面这一事实——即地表之下延展着诸多地域，那里栖居着的生灵，纵然与上界不同，在机体的一切主要方面仍与上界生灵相近——的过程中，最令我困惑的，莫过于此事与一条学说之间的抵牾；我相信，多数地质学家和哲学家都同意这条学说，即：虽说在我们这里，太阳是热量的伟大源泉，但我们越是深入地壳之下，热度便越是递增，据说，自地表以下五十英尺处起算，热度以每深入一英尺递增一度的比率上升。不过，虽然我所谈的这个部族的地界，在高地上距地表相对较近，使我能够解释那里何以有宜于有机生命的温度，但就连那个国度的沟壑与深谷，其热度也远低于哲学家们认为在那等深度下所能容许的程度——肯定不比法国南部，或者说至少不比意大利更热。而据我所闻的一切记述，在比这深不可测得多的地下广袤地域——人们本会以为唯有火蜥蜴才能存活于彼处——竟栖居着无数构造与我们相类的种族。这一事实与公认的科学法则如此相悖，我绝不敢妄称能加以解释；琪（Zee）也未能给我多少解决此谜的助力。她只是揣测，我们的哲学家对地球内部的极端多孔——其空穴与凹凸浩大无边，足以造成畅行的气流与频繁的风——以及对热量赖以蒸发散逸的种种途径，都未曾作充分的估量。不过她承认，在某个深度以下，热被认为非弗里尔-雅人（the Vril-ya）经验所知的这类有机生命所能忍受；虽然如此，他们的哲学家相信，即便在那等处所，也会有某种生命——有感觉的生命、有智性的生命——繁盛兴旺，只要哲学家们能够深入其间。“凡至善者（the All-Good）营造居所之处，”她说，“请确信，他必在那里安置居民。他不喜欢空寂的居所。”不过她补充说，弗里尔-雅人凭其技艺已使气温与气候发生了诸多改变，而弗里尔（vril）之力在这些改变中屡奏其功。她描述了一种精微而赋人以生机的介质，名为莱（Lai），我疑心它与卢因斯博士（Dr. Lewins）的以太氧本是同物；凡以弗里尔之名统摄起来的种种相协之力，正是在这种介质中运作。她力言，无论何处，只要这种介质能被——不妨如此说——充分扩展，足以让弗里尔的种种作用从容施展，那里便能获得一种宜于生命之最高形态的温度。她还说，他们的博物学家相信，花卉草木最初（无论是从大自然早期剧变时由地表携带下来的种子发育而成，还是由最先遁入洞窟的诸部族携入）皆是靠不断施于其上的光线的作用，加之栽培的逐渐改良，方才生成。她又说，自从弗里尔之光取代了一切其他发光之物，花与叶的颜色变得愈加鲜明，草木的生长也愈加高大。

这些问题，姑且留待更有能力处置它们的人去考量；现在，我必须以数页的篇幅，专门谈谈与弗里尔-雅人语言相关的那些极为有趣的问题。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
The language of the Vril-ya is peculiarly interesting, because it seems to me to exhibit with great clearness the traces of the three main transitions through which language passes in attaining to perfection of form.

One of the most illustrious of recent philologists, Max Müller, in arguing for the analogy between the strata of language and the strata of the earth, lays down this absolute dogma: “No language can, by any possibility, be inflectional without having passed through the agglutinative and isolating stratum. No language can be agglutinative without clinging with its roots to the underlying stratum of isolation.” —﻿*On the Stratification of Language*, p. 20.

Taking then the Chinese language as the best existing type of the original isolating stratum, “as the faithful photograph of man in his leading-strings trying the muscles of his mind, groping his way, and so delighted with his first successful grasps that he repeats them again and again,”2﻿—we have, in the language of the Vril-ya, still “clinging with its roots to the underlying stratum,” the evidences of the original isolation. It abounds in monosyllables, which are the foundations of the language. The transition into the agglutinative form marks an epoch that must have gradually extended through ages, the written literature of which has only survived in a few fragments of symbolical mythology and certain pithy sentences which have passed into popular proverbs. With the extant literature of the Vril-ya the inflectional stratum commences. No doubt at that time there must have operated concurrent causes, in the fusion of races by some dominant people, and the rise of some great literary phenomena by which the form of language became arrested and fixed. As the inflectional stage prevailed over the agglutinative, it is surprising to see how much more boldly the original roots of the language project from the surface that conceals them. In the old fragments and proverbs of the preceding stage the monosyllables which compose those roots vanish amidst words of enormous length, comprehending whole sentences from which no one part can be disentangled from the other and employed separately. But when the inflectional form of language became so far advanced as to have its scholars and grammarians, they seem to have united in extirpating all such polysynthetical or polysyllabic monsters, as devouring invaders of the aboriginal forms. Words beyond three syllables became proscribed as barbarous, and in proportion as the language grew thus simplified it increased in strength, in dignity, and in sweetness. Though now very compressed in sound, it gains in clearness by that compression. By a single letter, according to its position, they contrive to express all that with civilised nations in our upper world it takes the waste, sometimes of syllables, sometimes of sentences, to express. Let me here cite one or two instances: *An* (which I will translate man), *Ana* (men); the letter *s* is with them a letter implying multitude, according to where it is placed; *Sana* means mankind; *Ansa*, a multitude of men. The prefix of certain letters in their alphabet invariably denotes compound significations. For instance, *Gl* (which with them is a single letter, as *th* is a single letter with the Greeks) at the commencement of a word infers an assemblage or union of things, sometimes kindred, sometimes dissimilar﻿—as *Oon*, a house; *Gloon*, a town (i.e., an assemblage of houses). *Ata* is sorrow; *Glata*, a public calamity. *Aur-an* is the health or well-being of a man; *Glauran*, the well-being of the state, the good of the community; and a word constantly in ther mouths is *A-glauran*, which denotes their political creed﻿—viz., that “the first principle of a community is the good of all.” *Aub* is invention; *Sila*, a tone in music. *Glaubsila*, as uniting the ideas of invention and of musical intonation, is the classical word for poetry﻿—abbreviated, in ordinary conversation, to *Glaubs*. *Na*, which with them is, like *Gl*, but a single letter, always, when an initial, implies something antagonistic to life or joy or comfort, resembling in this the Aryan root *Nak*, expressive of perishing or destruction. *Nax* is darkness; *Narl*, death; *Naria*, sin or evil. *Nas*﻿—an uttermost condition of sin and evil﻿—corruption. In writing, they deem it irreverent to express the Supreme Being by any special name. He is symbolized by what may be termed the hieroglyphic of a pyramid, *A*. In prayer they address Him by a name which they deem too sacred to confide to a stranger, and I know it not. In conversation they generally use a periphrastic epithet, such as the All-Good. The letter *V*, symbolical of the inverted pyramid, where it is an initial, nearly always denotes excellence or power; as *Vril*, of which I have said so much; *Veed*, an immortal spirit; *Veed-ya*, immortality; *Koom*, pronounced like the Welsh *Cwm*, denotes something of hollowness. *Koom* itself is a cave; *Koom-in*, a hole; *Zi-koom*, a valley; *Koom-zi*, vacancy or void; *Bodh-koom*, ignorance (literally, knowledge-void). *Koom-Posh* is their name for the government of the many, or the ascendancy of the most ignorant or hollow. *Posh* is an almost untranslatable idiom, implying, as the reader will see later, contempt. The closest rendering I can give to it is our slang term, “bosh;” and thus *Koom-Posh* may be loosely rendered “Hollow-Bosh.” But when Democracy or *Koom-Posh* degenerates from popular ignorance into that popular passion or ferocity which precedes its decease, as (to cite illustrations from the upper world) during the French Reign of Terror, or for the fifty years of the Roman Republic preceding the ascendancy of Augustus, their name for that state of things is *Glek-Nas*. *Ek* is strife﻿—*Glek*, the universal strife. *Nas*, as I before said, is corruption or rot; thus *Glek-Nas* may be construed, “the universal strife-rot.” Their compounds are very expressive; thus, *Bodh* being knowledge, and *Too* a participle that implies the action of cautiously approaching﻿—*Too-bodh* is their word for Philosophy; *Pah* is a contemptuous exclamation analogous to our idiom, “stuff and nonsense;” *Pah-bodh* (literally stuff-and-nonsense-knowledge) is their term for futile or false philosophy, and applied to a species of metaphysical or speculative ratiocination formerly in vogue, which consisted in making inquiries that could not be answered, and were not worth making; such, for instance, as “Why does an *An* have five toes to his feet instead of four or six? Did the first *An*, created by the All-Good, have the same number of toes as his descendants? In the form by which an *An* will be recognised by his friends in the future state of being, will he retain any toes at all, and, if so, will they be material toes or spiritual toes?” I take these illustrations of *Pah-bodh*, not in irony or jest, but because the very inquiries I name formed the subject of controversy by the latest cultivators of that “science”﻿—4,000 years ago.

In the declension of nouns I was informed that anciently there were eight cases (one more than in the Sanskrit Grammar); but the effect of time has been to reduce these cases, and multiply, instead of these varying terminations, explanatory propositions. At present, in the Grammar submitted to my study, there were four cases to nouns, three having varying terminations, and the fourth a differing prefix.

An

Man

Ana

Men

Ano

to Man

Anoi

to Men

Anan

Man

Ananda

Men

Hil-An

O Man

Hil-Ananda

O Men

In the elder inflectional literature the dual form existed﻿—it has long been obsolete.

The genitive case with them is also obsolete; the dative supplies its place: they say the House *to* a Man, instead of the House *of* a Man. When used (sometimes in poetry), the genitive in the termination is the same as the nominative; so is the ablative, the preposition that marks it being a prefix or suffix at option, and generally decided by ear, according to the sound of the noun. It will be observed that the prefix *Hil* marks the vocative case. It is always retained in addressing another, except in the most intimate domestic relations; its omission would be considered rude: just as in our old forms of speech in addressing a king it would have been deemed disrespectful to say “King,” and reverential to say “O King.” In fact, as they have no titles of honour, the vocative adjuration supplies the place of a title, and is given impartially to all. The prefix *Hil* enters into the composition of words that imply distant communications, as *Hil-ya*, to travel.

In the conjugation of their verbs, which is much too lengthy a subject to enter on here, the auxiliary verb *Ya*, “to go,” which plays so considerable part in the Sanskrit, appears and performs a kindred office, as if it were a radical in some language from which both had descended. But another auxiliary of opposite signification also accompanies it and shares its labours﻿—viz., *Zi*, to stay or repose. Thus *Ya* enters into the future tense, and *Zi* in the preterite of all verbs requiring auxiliaries. *Yam*, I go﻿—*Yiam*, I may go﻿—*Yani-ya*, I shall go (literally, I go to go), *Zam-poo-yan*, I have gone (literally, I rest from gone). *Ya*, as a termination, implies by analogy, progress, movement, efflorescence. *Zi*, as a terminal, denotes fixity, sometimes in a good sense, sometimes in a bad, according to the word with which it is coupled. *Iva-zi*, eternal goodness; *Nan-zi*, eternal evil. *Poo* (from) enters as a prefix to words that denote repugnance, or things from which we ought to be averse. *Poo-pra*, disgust; *Poo-naria*, falsehood, the vilest kind of evil. *Poosh* or *Posh* I have already confessed to be untranslatable literally. It is an expression of contempt not unmixed with pity. This radical seems to have originated from inherent sympathy between the labial effort and the sentiment that impelled it, *Poo* being an utterance in which the breath is exploded from the lips with more or less vehemence. On the other hand, *Z*, when an initial, is with them a sound in which the breath is sucked inward, and thus *Zu*, pronounced *Zoo* (which in their language is one letter), is the ordinary prefix to words that signify something that attracts, pleases, touches the heart﻿—as *Zummer*, lover; *Zutze*, love; *Zuzulia*, delight. This indrawn sound of *Z* seems indeed naturally appropriate to fondness. Thus, even in our language, mothers say to their babies, in defiance of grammar, “Zoo darling;” and I have heard a learned professor at Boston call his wife (he had been only married a month) “Zoo little pet.”

I cannot quit this subject, however, without observing by what slight changes in the dialects favoured by different tribes of the same race, the original signification and beauty of sounds may become confused and deformed. Zee told me with much indignation that *Zummer* (lover) which, in the way she uttered it, seemed slowly taken down to the very depths of her heart, was, in some not very distant communities of the Vril-ya, vitiated into the half-hissing, half-nasal, wholly disagreeable, sound of *Subber*. I thought to myself it only wanted the introduction of *n* before *u* to render it into an English word significant of the last quality an amorous *Gy* would desire in her *Zummer*.

I will but mention another peculiarity in this language which gives equal force and brevity to its forms of expressions.

*A* is with them, as with us, the first letter of the alphabet, and is often used as a prefix word by itself to convey a complex idea of sovereignty or chiefdom, or presiding principle. For instance, *Iva* is goodness; *Diva*, goodness and happiness united; *A-Diva* is unerring and absolute truth. I have already noticed the value of *A* in *A-glauran*, so, in *vril* (to whose properties they trace their present state of civilisation), *A-vril* denotes, as I have said, civilisation itself.

The philologist will have seen from the above how much the language of the Vril-ya is akin to the Aryan or Indo-Germanic; but, like all languages, it contains words and forms in which transfers from very opposite sources of speech have been taken. The very title of *Tur*, which they give to their supreme magistrate, indicates theft from a tongue akin to the Turanian. They say themselves that this is a foreign word borrowed from a title which their historical records show to have been borne by the chief of a nation with whom the ancestors of the Vril-ya were, in very remote periods, on friendly terms, but which has long become extinct, and they say that when, after the discovery of *vril*, they remodelled their political institutions, they expressly adopted a title taken from an extinct race and a dead language for that of their chief magistrate, in order to avoid all titles for that office with which they had previous associations.

Should life be spared to me, I may collect into systematic form such knowledge as I acquired of this language during my sojourn amongst the Vril-ya. But what I have already said will perhaps suffice to show to genuine philological students that a language which, preserving so many of the roots in the aboriginal form, and clearing from the immediate, but transitory, polysynthetical stage so many rude incumbrances, has attained to such a union of simplicity and compass in its final inflectional forms, must have been the gradual work of countless ages and many varieties of mind; that it contains the evidence of fusion between congenial races, and necessitated, in arriving at the shape of which I have given examples, the continuous culture of a highly thoughtful people.

That, nevertheless, the literature which belongs to this language is a literature of the past; that the present felicitous state of society at which the Ana have attained forbids the progressive cultivation of literature, especially in the two main divisions of fiction and history﻿—I shall have occasion to show later.

===Chinese===
弗里尔-雅人的语言殊为有趣，因为在我看来，它极其清晰地显示出语言在臻于形式之完美的过程中所经历的三次主要变迁的痕迹。

近代最负盛名的语文学家之一马克斯·缪勒，在论证语言的地层与土地的地层之间的类比时，立下了这条绝对的教条：“任何语言，都绝无可能未经胶着层与孤立层而径直成为屈折语。任何语言，也绝无可能身为胶着语，其词根却不依附于其下方的孤立层。”——《论语言的地层》（*On the Stratification of Language*），第 20 页。

既然把汉语当作原始孤立层现存最好的类型，当作“人类尚系着学步襻带之时的一幅忠实照片：他试着活动心智的筋肉，一路摸索，又因最初几次抓握得手而如此欢喜，以至于一遍又一遍地重复它们”2——那么，在弗里尔-雅人的语言里，我们便看到它仍“以词根依附于其下的地层”，保有原始孤立状态的证据。这种语言富于单音节词，它们正是全语言的根基。向胶着形式的过渡标志着一个时代，那个时代必定渐渐绵延过许多岁月，其书面文学仅存于象征神话的零篇断简，和若干已化入民间谚语的精炼语句之中。弗里尔-雅人的现存文学，便是从屈折层开始的。毫无疑问，那时必有种种原因并作：某个占支配地位的民族促成诸族的融合，又有某种伟大的文学现象兴起，使语言的形式就此凝定。及至屈折阶段胜过胶着阶段，令人惊异的是，语言的原始词根竟何等鲜明地从掩蔽它们的表层中突露出来。而在前一阶段的古老断片与谚语里，构成那些词根的单音节词消没在极其冗长的词语之中——那些词语容纳整个句子，其中任何一部分都无法同别的部分拆解开来单独使用。但等到屈折形式长进到自有其学者与文法家的地步，他们似乎曾同心协力，把这一切多式综合的、多音节的怪物尽数铲除，视之为吞食原初形式的入侵之敌。超过三个音节的词遂遭摈弃，目为野蛮；而语言既经这般简化，便也相应地在气力、尊严与甜美上日益增长。它如今音响极为紧凑，却正由这紧凑而赢得清晰。凭单独一个字母，视其位置而定，他们便能表达出我们上界文明民族须得耗费——有时是音节，有时是整句——才能表达的一切。请容我在此举一两个例子：*安*（An，我姑且译作男子），*安-纳*（Ana，众男子）；字母 *s* 在他们是一种含示众多的字母，视其所在位置而定；*Sana* 意为人类；*Ansa* 则是一大群男子。他们字母表中某些字母的前缀，一律表示复合的意义。例如 *Gl*（在他们是一个单独的字母，一如 *th* 在希腊人是一个单独的字母）置于词首，便含示事物的聚集或联合，有时是同类的，有时是异类的——如 *Oon*，房屋；*Gloon*，市镇（即房屋的聚集）。*Ata* 是忧苦；*Glata* 是公共的灾殃。*Aur-an* 是一个人的健康或安乐；*Glauran* 是国家的安乐、社会的公益；而他们口中常挂着的一个词是 *A-glauran*，它表明他们的政治信条——即“社会的第一原则是全体之善”。*Aub* 是发明；*Sila* 是一个乐音。*Glaubsila* 兼含发明与乐音两义，是诗歌的雅言——在日常谈话中略作 *Glaubs*。*Na* 在他们也同 *Gl* 一样只是单独一个字母，凡居词首，必含示与生命、欢愉或安适相敌之物，这一点类似雅利安词根 *Nak*——表灭亡或毁坏之意的。*Nax* 是黑暗；*Narl* 是死亡；*Naria* 是罪或恶。*Nas* 是罪与恶的极境——腐坏。在书写中，他们认为以任何专名指称至上者都属不敬。祂以一座金字塔的、可称为象形文字的 *A* 来象征。祷告时他们以一个名称呼祂——那名称他们视为过于神圣，不可告知外人，我因而无从知晓。言谈之间，他们一般用一种迂回的称谓，如至善者。字母 *V* 象征倒置的金字塔，凡居词首，几乎总表示卓越或力量：如*弗里尔*（Vril），关于它我已说了这么多；*Veed*，不死的精灵；*Veed-ya*，不朽。*Koom*（发音像威尔士语的 *Cwm*）表示某种中空之物。*Koom* 本身是洞穴；*Koom-in* 是孔穴；*Zi-koom* 是山谷；*Koom-zi* 是空虚或空无；*Bodh-koom* 是无知（字面义为“知识之空”）。库姆-波什（Koom-Posh）是他们给多数人之治、即最无知、最空洞者的优势地位所起的名称。*Posh* 几乎是一个无法移译的习语，含着——读者往后会看到——轻蔑之意。我所能给出的最近似的译法，是我们俚语中的“bosh”（胡话）；因此库姆-波什不妨宽泛地译作“空洞-胡话”（Hollow-Bosh）。但当民主制或库姆-波什从民众的无知堕入民众的激情或狂暴——那正是它临终之前的征象——例如（且从上界举例）在法国恐怖统治时期，或在奥古斯都得势之前罗马共和国的那五十年间，他们对那种局面的名称是 *Glek-Nas*。*Ek* 是争斗——*Glek* 是普遍的争斗。*Nas*，如我先前所说，是腐坏或朽烂；因此 *Glek-Nas* 可以解作“普遍的争斗之朽”。他们的复合词极富表现力；例如 *Bodh* 是知识，*Too* 是一个表示谨慎趋近之动作的分词——*Too-bodh* 便是他们称为哲学的词；*Pah* 是一声轻蔑的感叹，相当于我们的习语“胡说与废话”；*Pah-bodh*（字面义为胡说-废话-知识）是他们指无益或虚妄之哲学的术语，专指从前盛行的一种玄学的或思辨的推理，其法在于提出既无从回答、也不值得提出的问题；例如：“一个*安*的脚上为何有五根足趾，而不是四根或六根？至善者所造的第一个*安*，足趾的数目是否与其后裔相同？在来世朋友赖以认出一个*安*的那个形体里，他究竟还保不保留足趾？倘若保留，那足趾是物质的足趾，还是精神的足趾？”我举这些 *Pah-bodh* 的例子，并非出于讥讽或戏谑，而是因为我所点名的那些问题，恰是那种“科学”晚近的耕耘者激烈争辩过的题目——在 4,000 年前。

据告知，在名词的变格上，古时有八个格（比梵文文法多一个）；但时间的作用却是削减这些格，并且不去增多变化的词尾，反而增多解释性的词语。在提交我研习的那部文法里，名词现有四格：三格以变化的词尾相区别，第四格则以相异的前缀相区别。

安（An）

男子

安-纳（Ana）

众男子

安诺（Ano）

予男子

安诺伊（Anoi）

予众男子

安南（Anan）

男子

安南达（Ananda）

众男子

希尔-安（Hil-An）

男子啊

希尔-安南达（Hil-Ananda）

众男子啊

在较早的屈折文学里，双数形式尚存——如今早已废弃。

他们的属格也已废弃，由与格代行其职：他们说“予某人的房屋”，而不说“某人的房屋”。属格偶一使用时（有时见于诗中），其词尾与主格相同；夺格亦然，标记它的虚词或作前缀、或作后缀，随人所欲，一般凭耳朵依名词的音响决定。可以看出，前缀 *Hil* 标记的是呼格。向人致辞时它总须保留，唯至亲密友的家庭关系间除外；省去它便会被视为失礼：正如在我们古时的言辞里，向君王致辞，直呼“王”会被认为不敬，称“王啊”方为恭敬。事实上，他们既无荣衔尊号，呼格的吁请便代行了头衔之职，并且不分彼此地施于一切人。前缀 *Hil* 还进入表示远隔交通之词语的构成，如 *Hil-ya*，旅行。

在他们动词的变位里——这个题目太长，此处无从细述——助动词 *Ya*（“去”）出现了，并且行使着一种与它在梵文中颇相类似的职分，仿佛它是某种语言的一个词根，而两种语言都自那语言递降而来。但另一个意义相反的助动词也与它相伴、分担其劳——即 *Zi*，停留或安息。于是 *Ya* 进入将来时，而 *Zi* 进入一切须用助动词的动词的过去时。*Yam*，我去——*Yiam*，我或许去——*Yani-ya*，我将去（字面义为“我去以去”）——*Zam-poo-yan*，我已去（字面义为“我自所去者而息”）。*Ya* 作为词尾，由类比而含示前进、运动、绽发；*Zi* 作为词尾，则表示凝定，或作褒解，或作贬解，视与其相配的词而定。*Iva-zi*，永恒之善；*Nan-zi*，永恒之恶。*Poo*（自、从）作为前缀进入表示嫌恶的词，或我们理应厌避的事物。*Poo-pra*，憎厌；*Poo-naria*，虚妄，恶中之最卑者。*Poosh* 或 *Posh*，我已承认无法直译。它是一种不无怜悯的轻蔑之辞。这个词根似乎源起于唇部发力与驱迫它的情感之间的固有感应：*Poo* 便是一种把气息或多或少带些猛劲从唇间迸出的发音。另一方面，*Z* 居词首时，在他们是一种把气息向内吸入的声音，于是 *Zu*——读作 *Zoo*（在他们的语言里是一个字母）——便是表示吸引人、怡悦人、动人之物的词的寻常前缀——如 *Zummer*，钟情人；*Zutze*，爱；*Zuzulia*，欢悦。*Z* 这一内吸的音，看来确实天然宜于柔情。甚至在我们自己的语言里，母亲们对婴儿也置文法于不顾，说“Zoo darling”（乖囡）；我还听波士顿一位博学的教授唤他的妻子（他们新婚方一月）“Zoo little pet”（小宝贝儿）。

然而，在离开这个题目之前，我不能不指出：同一种族不同部族所偏用的方言，只消发生轻微的变化，便足以使语音原有的意义与美紊乱而走形。琪愤愤然告诉我，*Zummer*（钟情人）这个词——照她发音的方式，仿佛被缓缓沉入她心底最深处——在弗里尔-雅人某些相距不很远的社会里，竟败坏成了 *Subber* 那种半带嘶声、半带鼻音、全然不悦耳的音响。我心里想，只消在 *u* 前添一个 *n*，它便成了一个英文词，所指的恰是多情的*姬*最不愿她的 *Zummer* 具有的最后一种品质。

我只想再提这种语言的另一个特点：它使其表达的形式兼具力量与简洁。

*A* 在他们，一如在我们，是字母表的首字母，且常单独用作前缀词，传达主权、首领之位或宰制原则的复合观念。例如 *Iva* 是善；*Diva* 是善与幸福之合；*A-Diva* 是无误而绝对的真理。我已提过 *A* 在 *A-glauran* 一词中的价值；同样，在*弗里尔*（他们把自己现今的文明状态溯源于其诸特性）中，*A-vril* 便如我所说，意指文明本身。

语文学家由上文可以看出，弗里尔-雅人的语言与雅利安语即印度-日耳曼语何等相近；但同一切语言一样，它也含有从截然相反的言语来源移借而来的词与形式。他们授予最高长官的*图尔*（Tur）这个头衔本身，便表明是从一种与图兰语相近的语言中窃来的。他们自己说，这是一个外来词，借自一个头衔——他们的史册表明，那头衔曾为一个民族的首领所佩戴；在极遥远的时代，弗里尔-雅人的祖先与那个民族交好，而那个民族久已灭绝；他们又说，自从发现*弗里尔*之后，他们重塑其政治制度之时，特地为他们最高长官选了这个取自绝种之族、死亡之语的头衔，以免此职染有任何令他们旧有联想的头衔。

倘若天假我年，我或将把旅居弗里尔-雅人中间时所获的这种语言的知识，整理成系统的形式。但我已说过的这些，或许已足以向真正的语文学学生表明：一种语言，既在原初形态中保存了这么多词根，又从那紧邻的、但转瞬即逝的多式综合阶段清除了这么多粗陋的赘累，而其最终的屈折形式又达到了如此简洁与容量的结合，那它必定是无数时代、无数种心智的渐进之功；它含着同气诸族融合的证据，而且，要达到我举例的那些形状，便必须有一个人深思远的民族持续不断的栽培。

然而，属于这种语言的文学乃是一种过去的文学；安-纳人所达到的当今这种幸福的社会状态，禁绝了文学的进步耕耘，尤其是在小说与历史这两大部门——这一点，我以后还有机会表明。

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
This people have a religion, and, whatever may be said against it, at least it has these strange peculiarities: firstly, that all believe in the creed they profess; secondly, that they all practice the precepts which the creed inculcates. They unite in the worship of one divine Creator and Sustainer of the universe. They believe that it is one of the properties of the all-permeating agency of *vril*, to transmit to the wellspring of life and intelligence every thought that a living creature can conceive; and though they do not contend that the idea of a Deity is innate, yet they say that the *An* (man) is the only creature, so far as their observation of nature extends, to whom *the capacity of conceiving that idea*, with all the trains of thought which open out from it, is vouchsafed. They hold that this capacity is a privilege that cannot have been given in vain, and hence that prayer and thanksgiving are acceptable to the divine Creator, and necessary to the complete development of the human creature. They offer their devotions both in private and public. Not being considered one of their species, I was not admitted into the building or temple in which the public worship is rendered; but I am informed that the service is exceedingly short, and unattended with any pomp of ceremony. It is a doctrine with the Vril-ya, that earnest devotion or complete abstraction from the actual world cannot, with benefit to itself, be maintained long at a stretch by the human mind, especially in public, and that all attempts to do so either lead to fanaticism or to hypocrisy. When they pray in private, it is when they are alone or with their young children.

They say that in ancient times there was a great number of books written upon speculations as to the nature of the Deity, and upon the forms of belief or worship supposed to be most agreeable to Him. But these were found to lead to such heated and angry disputations as not only to shake the peace of the community and divide families before the most united, but in the course of discussing the attributes of the Deity, the existence of the Deity Himself became argued away, or, what was worse, became invested with the passions and infirmities of the human disputants. “For,” said my host, “since a finite being like an *An* cannot possibly define the Infinite, so, when he endeavours to realise an idea of the Divinity, he only reduces the Divinity into an *An* like himself.” During the later ages, therefore, all theological speculations, though not forbidden, have been so discouraged as to have fallen utterly into disuse.

The Vril-ya unite in a conviction of a future state, more felicitous and more perfect than the present. If they have very vague notions of the doctrine of rewards and punishments, it is perhaps because they have no systems of rewards and punishments among themselves, for there are no crimes to punish, and their moral standard is so even that no *An* among them is, upon the whole, considered more virtuous than another. If one excels, perhaps, in one virtue, another equally excels in some other virtue; if one has his prevalent fault or infirmity, so also another has his. In fact, in their extraordinary mode of life, there are so few temptations to wrong, that they are good (according to their notions of goodness) merely because they live. They have some fanciful notions upon the continuance of life, when once bestowed, even in the vegetable world, as the reader will see in the next chapter.

===Chinese===
这个民族自有其宗教，而且无论人们对它有何指摘，它至少有如下两个奇特之处：其一，人人皆笃信自己宣认的信条；其二，人人皆践行信条所训诲的箴规。他们共同崇拜一位神圣的宇宙创造者与养护者。他们相信，*弗里尔*（Vril）这种渗透万有的力量，其诸般特性之一，便是将每一个生灵所能想见的念头，都传送到生命与智慧的源泉那里；他们虽不主张神明的观念乃与生俱来，却说——就他们对自然的观察所及——唯独*安*（An，即男子）这一造物蒙受了恩赐，得以具有*构想那一观念的能力*，以及由此开启而出的种种思路。他们认为，这一能力是一项特权，断不会白白赐予；因此，祷告与感恩必为神圣的创造者所悦纳，亦为人这一造物的完全发展所必需。他们的敬拜兼行于私下与公开。我既不被视为他们的同类，便不得进入举行公共礼拜的那座建筑或神殿；但据我所闻，其礼拜仪式极为简短，不杂任何铺张的仪节。弗里尔-雅人中间有一条教义：热忱的敬拜，或者说对现实世界的全然超离，人心若求于己有益，便不能长久持续，在公众面前尤其如此；凡强行为之而欲持久者，非沦为狂热，即流于伪善。他们私下祷告时，不是独自一人，便是只与年幼的子女相处。

据说古时曾有大量书籍，论究神性的本质，以及据信最能取悦于神明的信仰形式或崇拜形式。但后来发现，这些书籍引发的争辩火气十足、怒意汹汹，不仅动摇了社会的安宁，使此前最为和睦的家庭也告分裂，而且在讨论神之属性的进程中，神自身的存在竟被论辩得烟消云散，或者更糟——神竟被赋予了论辩者本人的种种情欲与弱点。“因为，”我的主人说，“一个像*安*这样的有限之物，既然绝无可能界定无限者，那么他竭力去领解一个神性观念时，也不过是把神性贬降成一个像他自己一样的*安*罢了。”因此在后来的世代里，一切神学思辨虽未遭禁绝，却备受沮抑，以致完全废弃了。

弗里尔-雅人一致笃信有一个来世，它比今世更为幸福、更为完美。如果说他们对赏罚之说十分含糊，那或许是因为他们中间并没有赏罚的制度——既无罪行须加惩处，而他们的道德水准又如此均齐，以致总体而论，他们中间没有一个*安*被认为比另一个更有德行。倘使某人在某一种美德上见长，另一人便在另一种美德上同样见长；一人有其惯常的过失或弱点，另一人亦自有其弱点。事实上，在他们那种异乎寻常的生活方式里，作恶的诱惑如此之少，以至于他们之所以善良（按他们对善良的理解），仅仅因为他们活着而已。至于生命一经赋予便延续不绝——甚至在植物界亦然——他们还有些奇思异想，读者在下一章自会看到。

## XIV / 十四

===Original===
Though, as I have said, the Vril-ya discourage all speculations on the nature of the Supreme Being, they appear to concur in a belief by which they think to solve that great problem of the existence of evil which has so perplexed the philosophy of the upper world. They hold that wherever He has once given life, with the perceptions of that life, however faint it be, as in a plant, the life is never destroyed; it passes into new and improved forms, though not in this planet (differing therein from the ordinary doctrine of metempsychosis), and that the living thing retains the sense of identity, so that it connects its past life with its future, and is *conscious* of its progressive improvement in the scale of joy. For they say that, without this assumption, they cannot, according to the lights of human reason vouchsafed to them, discover the perfect justice which must be a constituent quality of the All-Wise and the All-Good. Injustice, they say, can only emanate from three causes: want of wisdom to perceive what is just, want of benevolence to desire, want of power to fulfill it; and that each of these three wants is incompatible in the All-Wise, the All-Good, the All-Powerful. But that, while even in this life, the wisdom, the benevolence, and the power of the Supreme Being are sufficiently apparent to compel our recognition, the justice necessarily resulting from those attributes, absolutely requires another life, not for man only, but for every living thing of the inferior orders. That, alike in the animal and the vegetable world, we see one individual rendered, by circumstances beyond its control, exceedingly wretched compared to its neighbours﻿—one only exists as the prey of another﻿—even a plant suffers from disease till it perishes prematurely, while the plant next to it rejoices in its vitality and lives out its happy life free from a pang. That it is an erroneous analogy from human infirmities to reply by saying that the Supreme Being only acts by general laws, thereby making his own secondary causes so potent as to mar the essential kindness of the First Cause; and a still meaner and more ignorant conception of the All-Good, to dismiss with a brief contempt all consideration of justice for the myriad forms into which He has infused life, and assume that justice is only due to the single product of the *An*. There is no small and no great in the eyes of the divine Life-Giver. But once grant that nothing, however humble, which feels that it lives and suffers, can perish through the series of ages, that all its suffering here, if continuous from the moment of its birth to that of its transfer to another form of being, would be more brief compared with eternity than the cry of the newborn is compared to the whole life of a man; and once suppose that this living thing retains its sense of identity when so transformed (for without that sense it could be aware of no future being), and though, indeed, the fulfilment of divine justice is removed from the scope of our ken, yet we have a right to assume it to be uniform and universal, and not varying and partial, as it would be if acting only upon general secondary laws; because such perfect justice flows of necessity from perfectness of knowledge to conceive, perfectness of love to will, and perfectness of power to complete it.

However fantastic this belief of the Vril-ya may be, it tends perhaps to confirm politically the systems of government which, admitting different degrees of wealth, yet establishes perfect equality in rank, exquisite mildness in all relations and intercourse, and tenderness to all created things which the good of the community does not require them to destroy. And though their notion of compensation to a tortured insect or a cankered flower may seem to some of us a very wild crotchet, yet, at least, it is not a mischievous one; and it may furnish matter for no unpleasing reflection to think that within the abysses of earth, never lit by a ray from the material heavens, there should have penetrated so luminous a conviction of the ineffable goodness of the Creator﻿—so fixed an idea that the general laws by which He acts cannot admit of any partial injustice or evil, and therefore cannot be comprehended without reference to their action over all space and throughout all time. And since, as I shall have occasion to observe later, the intellectual conditions and social systems of this subterranean race comprise and harmonise great, and apparently antagonistic, varieties in philosophical doctrine and speculation which have from time to time been started, discussed, dismissed, and have reappeared amongst thinkers or dreamers in the upper world﻿—so I may perhaps appropriately conclude this reference to the belief of the Vril-ya, that self-conscious or sentient life once given is indestructible among inferior creatures as well as in man, by an eloquent passage from the work of that eminent zoologist, Louis Agassiz, which I have only just met with, many years after I had committed to paper those recollections of the life of the Vril-ya which I now reduce into something like arrangement and form: “The relations which individual animals bear to one another are of such a character that they ought long ago to have been considered as sufficient proof that no organised being could ever have been called into existence by other agency than by the direct intervention of a reflective mind. This argues strongly in favour of the existence in every animal of an immaterial principle similar to that which by its excellence and superior endowments places man so much above the animals; yet the principle unquestionably exists, and whether it be called sense, reason, or instinct, it presents in the whole range of organised beings a series of phenomena closely linked together, and upon it are based not only the higher manifestations of the mind, but the very permanence of the specific differences which characterise every organism. Most of the arguments in favour of the immortality of man apply equally to the permanency of this principle in other living beings. May I not add that a future life in which man would be deprived of that great source of enjoyment and intellectual and moral improvement which results from the contemplation of the harmonies of an organic world would involve a lamentable loss? And may we not look to a spiritual concert of the combined worlds and *all* their inhabitants in the presence of their Creator as the highest conception of paradise?” —﻿*Essay on Classification*, sect. XVII, p. 97﻿–﻿99.

===Chinese===
弗里尔-雅人（the Vril-ya）虽如我所说，对一切关于至上者本质的思辨加以沮抑，他们却似乎一致信奉一种信念，并自以为借它能解决那个令上界哲学大为困扰的伟大难题——恶之存在的问题。他们主张：凡他一度赋予生命之处，只要那生命具有其知觉——无论何等微弱，如在一株植物中——那生命便永不毁灭；它过渡到新的、更完善的形态中去，只是并非在这颗行星之上（在这一点上，与寻常的灵魂转世学说不同），而且那活物保持着自我的同一之感，因而能将其前生与来世相连属，并对自己在幸福阶梯上的渐进改善有所自觉。因为他们说，若无这一假设，那么依照他们所蒙赐的人类理性之光，便无从发现那种必定构成全智者与至善者（the All-Good）之本质属性的完全正义。他们说，不义只能发于三种原因：缺乏洞见何为正义的智慧，缺乏欲求正义的仁爱，缺乏实现正义的权能；而这三种匮乏，每一种都与全智者、至善者、全能者不相容。但是，尽管即在此生之中，至上者的智慧、仁爱与权能也已昭然可见，足以强使我们承认，然而由这些属性必然生出的正义，却绝对需要另一个生命——不仅为人，也为下等诸类中的一切活物。即在动物界与植物界，我们都同样看见：某一个体因非其所能左右的环境而陷于极度的悲苦——一个个体仅仅作为另一个体的猎物而存在——连一株植物也会罹病，直到过早凋亡，而它旁边的那株，却以其生机欣欣自喜，无痛无恙地活满它幸福的一生。又说，若答称至上者只以普遍法则行事，从而使其自身的次因强大到足以损害第一因固有的仁慈，那是从人类自身的弱点引出的错误类比；而对至善者怀有更卑下、更无知的构想的，则是以轻蔑的一笔，打发掉对那千万种蒙他灌注了生命的形态的一切正义之思，并以为正义唯独应归于安（An）这独一的产物。在神圣的赋生者眼中，无所谓小，也无所谓大。但是，只要承认：凡是自觉活着并受苦之物，无论何等卑微，都不会在绵延的世代中消亡；它在此间的一切苦难，纵然从降生之刻一直延续到转往另一种存在形态之刻，与永恒相比，也比新生儿的啼哭与人的整整一生相比还要短暂；又只要假定：这活物在如此转化之际仍保持其自我的同一之感（因为没有那种感觉，它便无从知晓任何未来的存在），那么，尽管神之正义的实现确乎超出了我们见识的范围，我们却有权假定它是一律而遍及的，而非变动而偏狭的——倘若它只循一般的次因而行，便会是那样；因为这样的完全正义，必然发于构想它的完全之知、意愿它的完全之爱，以及成全它的完全之能。

弗里尔-雅人的这一信念无论何等离奇，它或许恰能在政治上巩固他们的政体——那政体承认财富的差等，却确立了品级上的完全平等、一切关系与交往中的温文宽和，以及对一切造物的仁慈——凡公众福祉不需要他们毁除的造物，无不如此。尽管他们那种要向受折磨的昆虫或罹病的花朵给予补偿的观念，在我们某些人看来或许是一个十分狂想的怪念头，但它至少不是有害的怪念头；而想到在大地深处的渊薮之中——那里从不曾被物质天宇的一缕光芒照亮——竟已透入了如此明澈的信念，确信造物主那难以言喻的良善，如此坚定的观念，确信他所循以行事的普遍法则不容任何偏狭的不义或邪恶，因而若不参照这些法则遍一切空间、贯一切时间的作用，便无从领会它们——想来亦足供一番并非不愉快的沉思。而且，由于——我往后将有机会指出——这个地下种族的智识状况与社会制度，涵纳并调和着种种重大的、表面上互相抵牾的哲学学说与思辨的变体，它们在上界时时被提出、被讨论、被摒弃，又在思想家或梦想者中间重新出现——所以，结束这篇关于弗里尔-雅人信念的论述——即自觉的或有知觉的生命一经赋予，在下等造物中一如在人中一样不可毁灭——时，引那位杰出动物学家路易·阿加西斯（Louis Agassiz）著作中一段雄文作结，或许正相合宜；这段文字，是在我把关于弗里尔-雅人生活的那些回忆写成文字——如今我正着手将它们略加整理编排成篇——之后多年，才刚刚遇到的：“各个动物彼此之间的关系，具有这样的性质：它们早理应被视为充分的证据，证明任何有机存在者，除了一颗能思之心的直接干预以外，都不可能由任何其他作用唤入存在。这有力地佐证了：每一个动物身上都存在一种非物质的本原，与那凭借其卓越与优厚的禀赋使人远远高出动物之上的本原相似；而这本原确然存在，无论它被称为感觉、理性还是本能，它在有机存在者的整个范围内呈现出一系列紧密相连的现象，不仅心智的种种高级显现以它为基础，连那刻画每一有机体之特征的种别差异的恒久性，也以它为基础。赞成人之不朽的大多数论据，同样适用于这一本原在其他生灵中的恒存。容我再加一句：来世之人若被剥夺了那重大的愉悦与智识道德进步的源泉——它源自对有机世界诸般和谐的观照——岂不意味着可悲的损失？而诸世界及其一切居民在造物主面前同奏的一场灵性合唱，我们岂不可将它视为对天堂的最高构想？”——《论分类》（Essay on Classification），第十七节，第 97–99 页。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
As we walked back to the town, Taë took a new and circuitous way, in order to show me what, to use a familiar term, I will call the “Station,” from which emigrants or travellers to other communities commence their journeys. I had, on a former occasion, expressed a wish to see their vehicles. These I found to be of two kinds, one for land journeys, one for aerial voyages: the former were of all sizes and forms, some not larger than an ordinary carriage, some movable houses of one story and containing several rooms, furnished according to the ideas of comfort or luxury which are entertained by the Vril-ya. The aerial vehicles were of light substances, not the least resembling our balloons, but rather our boats and pleasure-vessels, with helm and rudder, with large wings or paddles, and a central machine worked by *vril*. All the vehicles both for land or air were indeed worked by that potent and mysterious agency.

I saw a convoy set out on its journey, but it had few passengers, containing chiefly articles of merchandise, and was bound to a neighbouring community; for among all the tribes of the Vril-ya there is considerable commercial interchange. I may here observe, that their money currency does not consist of the precious metals, which are too common among them for that purpose. The smaller coins in ordinary use are manufactured from a peculiar fossil shell, the comparatively scarce remnant of some very early deluge, or other convulsion of nature, by which a species has become extinct. It is minute, and flat as an oyster, and takes a jewel-like polish. This coinage circulates among all the tribes of the Vril-ya. Their larger transactions are carried on much like ours, by bills of exchange, and thin metallic plates which answer the purpose of our banknotes.

Let me take this occasion of adding that the taxation among the tribe I became acquainted with was very considerable, compared with the amount of population. But I never heard that anyone grumbled at it, for it was devoted to purposes of universal utility, and indeed necessary to the civilisation of the tribe. The cost of lighting so large a range of country, of providing for emigration, of maintaining the public buildings at which the various operations of national intellect were carried on, from the first education of an infant to the departments in which the College of Sages were perpetually trying new experiments in mechanical science﻿—all these involved the necessity for considerable state funds. To these I must add an item that struck me as very singular. I have said that all the human labour required by the state is carried on by children up to the marriageable age. For this labour the state pays, and at a rate immeasurably higher than our own remuneration to labour even in the United States. According to their theory, every child, male or female, on attaining the marriageable age, and there terminating the period of labour, should have acquired enough for an independent competence during life. As, no matter what the disparity of fortune in the parents, all the children must equally serve, so all are equally paid according to their several ages or the nature of their work. Where the parents or friends choose to retain a child in their own service, they must pay into the public fund in the same ratio as the state pays to the children it employs; and this sum is handed over to the child when the period of service expires. This practice serves, no doubt, to render the notion of social equality familiar and agreeable; and if it may be said that all the children form a democracy, no less truly it may be said that all the adults form an aristocracy. The exquisite politeness and refinement of manners among the Vril-ya, the generosity of their sentiments, the absolute leisure they enjoy for following out their own private pursuits, the amenities of their domestic intercourse, in which they seem as members of one noble order that can have no distrust of each other’s word or deed, all combine to make the Vril-ya the most perfect nobility which a political disciple of Plato or Sidney could conceive for the ideal of an aristocratic republic.

===Chinese===
我们步行回城时，塔伊（Taë）特意取了一条新的迂回路径，为的是让我看一看一样去处——姑且用一个熟稔的字眼，我称之为“车站”——移往其他社群的移民或旅行者，便从那里启程。我先前曾表示想看看他们的载具。这些载具，我发现分作两类：一类供陆路行程，一类供空中航行。前者大小形制不一，有的不过一辆寻常马车大小，有的则是单层的可移动宅屋，内有数间屋子，其陈设之舒适或奢华，一依弗里尔-雅人（the Vril-ya）所怀的观念。空中载具以轻质材料制成，与我们的气球毫无相似之处，倒更近乎我们的舟艇与游船，配有舵柄与船舵、大翼或桨板，中央有一台以弗里尔（vril）驱动的机器。凡此陆行空游的一切载具，确实都由那种强大而神秘的力量驱动。

我看见一支结队的商旅启程上路，但乘客寥寥，装载的主要是货物，开往邻近的一个社群；因为在弗里尔-雅人的所有部族之间，商业往来相当可观。我不妨在此指出：他们的货币并不以贵金属为之，因为贵金属在他们中间太过常见，不堪此用。日常通用的小额钱币以一种奇特的化石贝壳制成——那是某次极为古远的洪水、或其他使一个物种灭绝的天变地异所遗下的、较为稀少的残壳。它细小，扁平如牡蛎，能磨出宝石般的光泽。这种钱币在弗里尔-雅人的所有部族间流通。他们的大宗交易，其进行方式与我们的大同小异：用汇票，以及起我们纸币之作用的薄金属板。

请容我借此机会补充一句：就我所结识的这个部族而论，其赋税与人口之众相比，颇为可观。但我从未听说有谁为此口出怨言，因为赋税皆用于普遍公益之事，而且确实为该部族的文明所必需。为如此广袤的一域国土照明的费用，为移民迁徙所备的经费，为维持那些公共建筑——民族智力的种种活动就在其中进行，从婴孩的启蒙教育，直到贤哲学院（College of Sages）不断以机械科学从事新实验的各个部门——所需的开销，凡此种种，都使相当可观的国库资金成为必需。在此之外，我还须添上一项令我感到十分奇特的开支。我说过，国家所需的全部人力劳作，概由未届婚龄的儿童承担。国家为这种劳动付酬，其酬率之高，即便与我们在美国付给劳动的报酬相比，也高出得无法计量。依照他们的理论，每个儿童，无论男女，到达婚龄、并于是终止劳役之时，都应已挣得足以终身自立营生的资财。因为无论父母家产如何悬殊，凡儿童皆须一律服役，故人人亦各按其年龄或工作之性质同样取酬。若父母或亲友愿意将一个孩子留在自己身边使唤，他们就必须按国家雇用儿童所付的同等比率，向公共基金缴款；服役期满，这笔款项便交到该儿童手中。这种做法无疑使社会平等的观念变得熟悉而可亲；倘若可以说全体儿童构成一个民主政体，那么同样真实地可以说，全体成人构成一个贵族政体。弗里尔-雅人举止的精致文雅、情操的宽宏慷慨、随心追求一己私务的绝对闲暇、家庭往来间的融洽敦睦——在其中，他们俨然是一个高贵阶层的成员，对彼此的言行不存半点猜疑——凡此种种合在一起，使弗里尔-雅人成为柏拉图或西德尼的政治门徒所能为贵族共和国的理想构想出的最完美的贵族阶层。

## XV / 十五

===Original===
Kind to me as I found all in this household, the young daughter of my host was the most considerate and thoughtful in her kindness. At her suggestion I laid aside the habiliments in which I had descended from the upper earth, and adopted the dress of the Vril-ya, with the exception of the artful wings which served them, when on foot, as a graceful mantle. But as many of the Vril-ya, when occupied in urban pursuits, did not wear these wings, this exception created no marked difference between myself and the race among which I sojourned, and I was thus enabled to visit the town without exciting unpleasant curiosity. Out of the household no one suspected that I had come from the upper world, and I was but regarded as one of some inferior and barbarous tribe whom Aph-Lin entertained as a guest.

The city was large in proportion to the territory round it, which was of no greater extent than many an English or Hungarian nobleman’s estate; but the whole of it, to the verge of the rocks which constituted its boundary, was cultivated to the nicest degree, except where certain allotments of mountain and pasture were humanely left free to the sustenance of the harmless animals they had tamed, though not for domestic use. So great is their kindness towards these humbler creatures, that a sum is devoted from the public treasury for the purpose of deporting them to other Vril-ya communities willing to receive them (chiefly new colonies), whenever they become too numerous for the pastures allotted to them in their native place. They do not, however, multiply to an extent comparable to the ratio at which, with us, animals bred for slaughter, increase. It seems a law of nature that animals not useful to man gradually recede from the domains he occupies, or even become extinct. It is an old custom of the various sovereign states amidst which the race of the Vril-ya are distributed, to leave between each state a neutral and uncultivated borderland. In the instance of the community I speak of, this tract, being a ridge of savage rocks, was impassable by foot, but was easily surmounted, whether by the wings of the inhabitants or the air-boats, of which I shall speak hereafter. Roads through it were also cut for the transit of vehicles impelled by *vril*. These intercommunicating tracts were always kept lighted, and the expense thereof defrayed by a special tax, to which all the communities comprehended in the denomination of Vril-ya contribute in settled proportions. By these means a considerable commercial traffic with other states, both near and distant, was carried on. The surplus wealth of this special community was chiefly agricultural. The community was also eminent for skill in constructing implements connected with the arts of husbandry. In exchange for such merchandise it obtained articles more of luxury than necessity. There were few things imported on which they set a higher price than birds taught to pipe artful tunes in concert. These were brought from a great distance, and were marvellous for beauty of song and plumage. I understand that extraordinary care was taken by their breeders and teachers in selection, and that the species had wonderfully improved during the last few years. I saw no other pet animals among this community except some very amusing and sportive creatures of the Batrachian species, resembling frogs, but with very intelligent countenances, which the children were fond of, and kept in their private gardens. They appear to have no animals akin to our dogs or horses, though that learned naturalist, Zee, informed me that such creatures had once existed in those parts, and might now be found in regions inhabited by other races than the Vril-ya. She said that they had gradually disappeared from the more civilised world since the discovery of *vril*, and the results attending that discovery had dispensed with their uses. Machinery and the invention of wings had superseded the horse as a beast of burden; and the dog was no longer wanted either for protection or the chase, as it had been when the ancestors of the Vril-ya feared the aggressions of their own kind, or hunted the lesser animals for food. Indeed, however, so far as the horse was concerned, this region was so rocky that a horse could have been, there, of little use either for pastime or burden. The only creature they use for the latter purpose is a kind of large goat which is much employed on farms. The nature of the surrounding soil in these districts may be said to have first suggested the invention of wings and air-boats. The largeness of space in proportion to the space occupied by the city, was occasioned by the custom of surrounding every house with a separate garden. The broad main street, in which Aph-Lin dwelt, expanded into a vast square, in which were placed the College of Sages and all the public offices; a magnificent fountain of the luminous fluid which I call naphtha (I am ignorant of its real nature) in the centre. All these public edifices have a uniform character of massiveness and solidity. They reminded me of the architectural pictures of Martin. Along the upper stories of each ran a balcony, or rather a terraced garden, supported by columns, filled with flowering plants, and tenanted by many kinds of tame birds. From the square branched several streets, all broad and brilliantly lighted, and ascending up the eminence on either side. In my excursions in the town I was never allowed to go alone; Aph-Lin or his daughter was my habitual companion. In this community the adult *Gy* is seen walking with any young *An* as familiarly as if there were no difference of sex.

===Chinese===
这户人家的人待我都甚友善，而主人家的年轻女儿，其好意的体贴与周到又是最深的。在她的提议下，我脱下了从上界下来时所穿的那身衣装，改穿了弗里尔-雅人（the Vril-ya）的服装，只是没有用他们那副精巧的双翼——步行时，双翼于他们乃是一件优美的披风。不过，弗里尔-雅人有许多在城市事务中忙碌的，并不佩这双翼，故而这一例外并未使我与我客居的这个种族之间生出显著的差别；我于是得以走访城中，而不致惹起令人不快的好奇。这户人家之外，无人疑心我是从上界来的；我只不过被当作某个低劣蛮野部落的一员——阿弗-林（Aph-Lin）待之为客而已。

这座城与四围疆域相比颇为宏大，而那疆域的广袤，也不过同英格兰或匈牙利许多贵族的领地相当；但其全部土地，直到构成边界的岩壁边缘为止，都耕治得精致入微，只有某些山地与草场的划块被仁厚地留置下来，任由那些无害的动物——他们驯养了它们，虽非供家用——自行觅食存活。他们对这些卑微生灵的仁慈如此深厚，以至于每当这些动物繁衍过多、故土划给它们的牧场不敷供养时，便从公共库府中拨出一笔款项，把它们遣送到愿意接收它们（主要是新辟的殖民地）的其他弗里尔-雅人社区去。然而它们的繁衍，并不至于同我们这里供屠宰而饲养的动物的增长比率相比拟。动物若于人无用，便逐渐退避出人所占据的地域，甚或归于灭绝——这似乎是自然的一条定律。弗里尔-雅这一种族分布所在的各个主权邦国之间，两两留有一带中立而未耕的边地，乃是一种古老的习俗。就我所说的这个社区而论，这条地带是一道荒岩的山脊，徒步无法通行，却轻易便可越过——或凭居民的翅膀，或凭飞舟，飞舟我往后还要谈到。其间也开凿了道路，供以弗里尔（vril）驱动的车辆往来。这些互相交通的道路经常保持照明，其费用由一项专门税课支付，凡在弗里尔-雅这一名目之下的所有社区，都按固定的比例捐输。凭借这些手段，与远近各邦开展了可观的商贸往来。这个特定社区的超额财富主要是农业性的。社区还以营造农艺器械的技艺著称。它以这类货品换取的物件，与其说供必需，不如说供奢华。进口货中难得有几样，比那种被教会合奏精巧曲调的鸣鸟更受他们珍视。这些鸟来自很远的地方，歌喉羽色俱极可观。据我所知，其饲养者与教师们在选育上费了非同寻常的心血，这一物种近几年来已有惊人的改良。在这个社区里，除了一些极为有趣、爱嬉戏的两栖类（Batrachian）生物——形似蛙，面相却甚聪慧，孩子们喜欢它们，养在自家的园子里——之外，我没有见过别的爱畜。他们似乎没有同我们的狗或马相近的动物，尽管那位博学的博物学家琪（Zee）告诉我，这样的生物从前在这一带地方确曾存在，如今或可在弗里尔-雅人以外其他种族所居的地域中找到。她说，自从发现弗里尔以来，它们便从较文明的世界里逐渐消失了，那一发现所带来的种种结果，使它们的用途成了多余。机械与双翼的发明，已取代了马作为驮兽的地位；狗也不再需要用来护卫或狩猎——从前弗里尔-雅人的祖先还畏惧同类的侵凌、或猎取小兽为食时，是用得着它的。不过说实在的，就马而论，这一带地域多岩，马在此地无论用于游娱或负重，本都没有多少用处。他们用于负重的唯一生物，是一种大山羊，农场上用得很多。这些地带四围土壤的性质，可以说最先启发了双翼与飞舟的发明。空地之广，与城池所占地面相比，其悬殊乃是由于每所宅子都环以一座独立花园的习俗。阿弗-林居住的那条宽阔大街，展开成一片广阔的广场，贤哲学院（College of Sages）和所有官署都设在广场上；中央有一座壮丽的喷泉，喷涌着那种我称之为石脑油的发光流体（它的真实性质我并不知道）。这些公共建筑一律具有厚重坚实的品格。它们使我想起马丁的建筑画。每座建筑的上层沿墙有一道游廊——不如说是一座阶梯园圃——由柱子支撑，满植花木，栖着许多种驯顺的鸟儿。从广场分出数条街道，全都宽阔而灯火辉朗，顺着两侧的高地徐徐上行。我在城中游历时，从不被准许独自前往；阿弗-林或他的女儿是我惯常的同伴。在这个社区里，成年的姬（Gy）与任何年轻的安（An）并肩同行，亲昵自然，仿佛两性之间不存在任何差异。

===Original===
The retail shops are not very numerous; the persons who attend on a customer are all children of various ages, and exceedingly intelligent and courteous, but without the least touch of importunity or cringing. The shopkeeper himself might or might not be visible; when visible, he seemed rarely employed on any matter connected with his professional business; and yet he had taken to that business from special liking to it, and quite independently of his general sources of fortune.

Some of the richest citizens in the community kept such shops. As I have before said, no difference of rank is recognizable, and therefore all occupations hold the same equal social status. An *An*, of whom I bought my sandals, was the brother of the *Tur*, or chief magistrate; and though his shop was not larger than that of any bootmaker in Bond Street or Broadway, he was said to be twice as rich as the *Tur* who dwelt in a palace. No doubt, however, he had some country-seat.

The *Ana* of the community are, on the whole, an indolent set of beings after the active age of childhood. Whether by temperament or philosophy, they rank repose among the chief blessings of life. Indeed, when you take away from a human being the incentives to action which are found in cupidity or ambition, it seems to me no wonder that he rests quiet.

===Chinese===
零售店铺并不很多；侍候顾客的都是各个年龄的孩子，极其聪敏有礼，却没有一丝强求纠缠或卑躬屈膝的气味。店主本人或见或不见；见到时，他也似乎难得忙于任何与他那门营生相关的事务；然而他从事那门营生，本是出于对它的特殊爱好，且与其一般的财源全然无涉。

社区里有些最富有的公民就开着这样的店铺。如我先前所说，这里辨不出等级的差异，因此一切职业都持有同等的社会地位。我向他买凉鞋的那位安，是图尔（Tur）——即行政长官——的兄弟；他的铺子虽不比邦德街或百老汇任何一家靴匠铺子大，据说却比那位住在宫殿里的图尔还要富上一倍。不过无疑，他总还有一处乡间宅邸。

这个社区的安-纳（the Ana），过了童年那个活跃的年纪之后，总体说来是一群懒散的生灵。无论出于气质还是出于哲理，他们都把安宁列为人生主要的福祉之一。的确，当你从一个人身上夺去贪婪与野心所供给的行动动力时，依我看，他安然而息，也就不足为奇了。

===Original===
In their ordinary movements they prefer the use of their feet to that of their wings. But for their sports or (to indulge in a bold misuse of terms) their public promenades, they employ the latter, also for the aerial dances I have described, as well as for visiting their country places, which are mostly placed on lofty heights; and, when still young, they prefer their wings for travel into the other regions of the Ana, to vehicular conveyances.

Those who accustom themselves to flight can fly, if less rapidly than some birds, yet from twenty-five to thirty miles an hour, and keep up that rate for five or six hours at a stretch. But the Ana generally, on reaching middle age, are not fond of rapid movements requiring violent exercise. Perhaps for this reason, as they hold a doctrine which our own physicians will doubtless approve﻿—viz., that regular transpiration through the pores of the skin is essential to health, they habitually use the sweating-baths to which we give the name of Turkish or Roman, succeeded by douches of perfumed waters. They have great faith in the salubrious virtue of certain perfumes.
