It is their custom also, at stated but rare periods, perhaps four times a year when in health, to use a bath charged with *vril*.3 They consider that this fluid, sparingly used, is a great sustainer of life; but used in excess, when in the normal state of health, rather tends to reaction and exhausted vitality. For nearly all their diseases, however, they resort to it as the chief assistant to nature in throwing off the complaint.

===Chinese===
日常行动中，他们宁用双脚而不用双翼。但在他们的游戏——或者（容我大胆误用一下名目）他们公开的散步游观——中，他们便用后者；在我描述过的那种空中舞蹈中也用；去他们的乡居也同样——那些乡居多设在高处；而且年纪尚轻时，往安-纳的其他地域旅行，他们也是宁用双翼而不乘车舆。

惯于飞行的人，飞起来虽比某些鸟类慢些，却也能每小时飞 25 至 30 英里，并且一口气保持这个速度五六个小时。但安-纳人一般到了中年，便不喜欢需要剧烈运动的快速动作了。或许正因如此——他们持有一条我们的医生想必也会赞成的学说，即：皮肤毛孔经常的发汗排泄于健康必不可少——他们惯常使用我们称之为土耳其式或罗马式的汗浴，浴后再以香芬之水浇注。他们对某些香气的保健功效极有信心。

他们另有一种习俗，在固定而稀少的时期——健康时或许每年四次——使用一次充注弗里尔〔3〕的浴。他们认为这种流体用之有节，乃是生命的一大支撑；但健康正常之时用之过度，反倒容易引起反应、耗竭生气。然而对于他们几乎一切的疾病，他们又都求助于它，视之为自然在祛除病患时的主要助力。

===Original===
In their own way they are the most luxurious of people, but all their luxuries are innocent. They may be said to dwell in an atmosphere of music and fragrance. Every room has its mechanical contrivances for melodious sounds, usually tuned down to soft-murmured notes, which seem like sweet whispers from invisible spirits. They are too accustomed to these gentle sounds to find them a hindrance to conversation, nor, when alone, to reflection. But they have a notion that to breathe an air filled with continuous melody and perfume has necessarily an effect at once soothing and elevating upon the formation of character and the habits of thought. Though so temperate, and with total abstinence from other animal food than milk, and from all intoxicating drinks, they are delicate and dainty to an extreme in food and beverage; and in all their sports even the old exhibit a childlike gaiety. Happiness is the end at which they aim, not as the excitement of a moment, but as the prevailing condition of the entire existence; and regard for the happiness of each other is evinced by the exquisite amenity of their manners.

Their conformation of skull has marked differences from that of any known races in the upper world, though I cannot help thinking it a development, in the course of countless ages of the Brachycephalic type of the Age of Stone in Lyell’s *Elements of Geology*, C. X., p. 113, as compared with the Dolichocephalic type of the beginning of the Age of Iron, correspondent with that now so prevalent amongst us, and called the Celtic type. It has the same comparative massiveness of forehead, not receding like the Celtic﻿—the same even roundness in the frontal organs; but it is far loftier in the apex, and far less pronounced in the hinder cranial hemisphere where phrenologists place the animal organs. To speak as a phrenologist, the cranium common to the Vril-ya has the organs of weight, number, tune, form, order, causality, very largely developed; that of construction much more pronounced than that of ideality. Those which are called the moral organs, such as conscientiousness and benevolence, are amazingly full; amativeness and combativeness are both small; adhesiveness large; the organ of destructiveness (i.e., of determined clearance of intervening obstacles) immense, but less than that of benevolence; and their philoprogenitiveness takes rather the character of compassion and tenderness to things that need aid or protection than of the animal love of offspring. I never met with one person deformed or misshapen. The beauty of their countenances is not only in symmetry of feature, but in a smoothness of surface, which continues without line or wrinkle to the extreme of old age, and a serene sweetness of expression, combined with that majesty which seems to come from consciousness of power and the freedom of all terror, physical or moral. It is that very sweetness, combined with that majesty, which inspired in a beholder like myself, accustomed to strive with the passions of mankind, a sentiment of humiliation, of awe, of dread. It is such an expression as a painter might give to a demigod, a genius, an angel. The males of the Vril-ya are entirely beardless; the *Gy-ei* sometimes, in old age, develop a small moustache.

===Chinese===
照他们自己的方式说来，他们是最奢华的人民，但他们的一切奢华都是无罪的。他们可以说居住在一片音乐与芬芳的氛围之中。每个房间都有发出乐音的机械装置，通常调得低柔如呢喃，仿佛不可见的精灵发出的甜美低语。他们对这些轻柔的声音习以为常，不觉得它们妨碍交谈，独处时也不觉得它们妨碍沉思。但他们有一种想法：呼吸着充满连续不断的旋律与香气的空气，必然对性格的形成与思维的习惯产生一种既安抚又升华的作用。他们虽然如此节制——除乳类以外完全戒绝别的肉食，并戒绝一切醉人的饮料——在饮食上却精细考究到了极点；而在他们的一切游戏中，连老人也露出孩子气的欢快。幸福便是他们所瞄准的鹄的——不是作为一时的兴奋，而是作为整个存在的一贯境况；而他们对彼此幸福的顾念，就显现在他们举止的无比温文之中。

他们的颅骨构造，与上界任何已知种族都有显著差异，尽管我不免认为，它是莱伊尔（Lyell）《地质学纲要》（Elements of Geology，第十章，第 113 页）中石器时代的圆颅型（Brachycephalic）类型在无数世纪间的发展——相对于铁器时代开始时的长颅型（Dolichocephalic）类型而言；后者与如今在我们中间如此盛行的那个类型相当，称为凯尔特型。它有同样相对厚重的额部，不像凯尔特型那样后倾；额部器官同样匀净圆整；但顶部远为高耸，颅后半球则远不那么突出——颅相学家把动物性器官安放在那里。以颅相学家的口吻说，弗里尔-雅人共同的颅骨，其重量、数、音调、形、秩序、因果性诸器官都极为发达；构造性远比理想性显著。那些被称为道德性器官的，如良心性与仁慈性，丰隆得惊人；恋爱性与好斗性都小；依恋性大；破坏性器官（即坚决清除障碍之官）极其巨大，但仍小于仁慈性；而他们的爱嗣性所取的性格，与其说是动物性的怜子之爱，不如说是对需要扶助或保护之物的怜悯与温柔。我从未遇见一个畸形或残缺的人。他们面容之美不仅在五官的匀称，而且在肌肤表面的平滑——直到极老之年也不生纹缕与皱纹——以及表情的恬静甜美，兼以一种似乎出于力量自觉、出于免除一切或身体或道德之恐怖的庄严。恰恰是这种甜美，与这种庄严相合，才在一个像我这样惯于同人类种种情欲搏斗的观者身上，唤起一种自惭、敬畏与悚惧之情。那是画家可能赋予半神、天才或天使的一种表情。弗里尔-雅人的男性全然无须；姬-埃（the Gy-ei）有时到老年来会长出一撇小小的髭须。

===Original===
I was surprised to find that the colour of their skin was not uniformly that which I had remarked in those individuals whom I had first encountered﻿—some being much fairer, and even with blue eyes, and hair of a deep golden auburn, though still of complexions warmer or richer in tone than persons in the north of Europe.

I was told that this admixture of colouring arose from intermarriage with other and more distant tribes of the Vril-ya, who, whether by the accident of climate or early distinction of race, were of fairer hues than the tribes of which this community formed one. It was considered that the dark-red skin showed the most ancient family of Ana; but they attached no sentiment of pride to that antiquity, and, on the contrary, believed their present excellence of breed came from frequent crossing with other families differing, yet akin; and they encourage such intermarriages, always provided that it be with the Vril-ya nations. Nations which, not conforming their manners and institutions to those of the Vril-ya, nor indeed held capable of acquiring the powers over the *vril* agencies which it had taken them generations to attain and transmit, were regarded with more disdain than the citizens of New York regard the negroes.

I learned from Zee, who had more lore in all matters than any male with whom I was brought into familiar converse, that the superiority of the Vril-ya was supposed to have originated in the intensity of their earlier struggles against obstacles in nature amidst the localities in which they had first settled. “Wherever,” said Zee, moralising, “wherever goes on that early process in the history of civilisation, by which life is made a struggle, in which the individual has to put forth all his powers to compete with his fellow, we invariably find this result﻿—viz., since in the competition a vast number must perish, nature selects for preservation only the strongest specimens. With our race, therefore, even before the discovery of *vril*, only the highest organisations were preserved; and there is among our ancient books a legend, once popularly believed, that we were driven from a region that seems to denote the world you come from, in order to perfect our condition and attain to the purest elimination of our species by the severity of the struggles our forefathers underwent; and that, when our education shall become finally completed, we are destined to return to the upper world, and supplant all the inferior races now existing therein.”

===Chinese===
我惊讶地发现，他们的肤色并不一律是我最初遇见的那几个人身上所见的颜色——有些人的肤色要白皙得多，甚至生着蓝眼睛和深金赤褐色的头发，不过其面色仍比北欧人温暖浓郁。

我被告知，这种颜色的混杂，来自与弗里尔-雅人其他更遥远部落的通婚——那些部落或因气候的偶然，或因种族的早期分化，肤色比组成这个社区的那些部落更白。人们认为，暗红的皮肤显示最古老的安-纳家族；但他们对此古老性不附任何自豪之感，反倒相信他们今日血统之优异，来自与别异而相近的家族的频繁杂交；他们鼓励这样的通婚，只要是与弗里尔-雅诸国通婚便始终如此。至于那些不使自己的风俗制度遵从弗里尔-雅人、也实在不被认为有能力获得对弗里尔种种作用之掌控的民族——那掌控是弗里尔-雅人花了世世代代才获得并传下的——他们看待这些民族，比纽约的公民看待黑人还要轻蔑。

我从琪那里得知——她所通晓的各方面的学问，比我与之亲昵交谈过的任何男性都多——弗里尔-雅人的优越，据认为起源于他们早期在他们最初定居的地方与自然障碍搏斗的剧烈。“凡是，”琪以说教的口吻说道，“凡是文明史上那种早期过程进行之处——生命借此成为一场搏斗，个人不得不倾其全力与同类竞争——我们总发现这样的结果：既然竞争中必有大批人归于灭亡，自然便只选择最强健的个体予以保留。因此，在我们的种族，甚至早在发现弗里尔之前，便只有最高等的机体得以保存；我们的古书中有一个传说，从前是众人相信的，说我们被从一个地方驱逐了出来——那地方似乎就是你所来自的世界——为的是借我们祖先所历经的搏斗之严酷，完善我们的境况，达到我们物种最纯粹的汰炼；还说，待我们的教养最终完成之时，我们注定要回到上界，取代如今存在于彼处的一切劣等种族。”

===Original===
Aph-Lin and Zee often conversed with me in private upon the political and social conditions of that upper world, in which Zee so philosophically assumed that the inhabitants were to be exterminated one day or other by the advent of the Vril-ya. They found in my accounts﻿—in which I continued to do all I could (without launching into falsehoods so positive that they would have been easily detected by the shrewdness of my listeners) to present our powers and ourselves in the most flattering point of view﻿—perpetual subjects of comparison between our most civilised populations and the meaner subterranean races which they considered hopelessly plunged in barbarism, and doomed to gradual if certain extinction. But they both agreed in desiring to conceal from their community all premature opening into the regions lighted by the sun; both were humane, and shrunk from the thought of annihilating so many millions of creatures; and the pictures I drew of our life, highly coloured as they were, saddened them. In vain I boasted of our great men﻿—poets, philosophers, orators, generals﻿—and defied the Vril-ya to produce their equals. “Alas!” said Zee, her grand face softening into an angel-like compassion, “this predominance of the few over the many is the surest and most fatal sign of a race incorrigibly savage. See you not that the primary condition of mortal happiness consists in the extinction of that strife and competition between individuals, which, no matter what forms of government they adopt, render the many subordinate to the few, destroy real liberty to the individual, whatever may be the nominal liberty of the state, and annul that calm of existence, without which, felicity, mental or bodily, cannot be attained? Our notion is, that the more we can assimilate life to the existence which our noblest ideas can conceive to be that of spirits on the other side of the grave, why, the more we approximate to a divine happiness here, and the more easily we glide into the conditions of being hereafter. For, surely, all we can imagine of the life of gods, or of blessed immortals, supposes the absence of self-made cares and contentious passions, such as avarice and ambition. It seems to us that it must be a life of serene tranquility, not indeed without active occupations to the intellectual or spiritual powers, but occupations, of whatsoever nature they be, congenial to the idiosyncrasies of each, not forced and repugnant﻿—a life gladdened by the untrammelled interchange of gentle affections, in which the moral atmosphere utterly kills hate and vengeance, and strife and rivalry. Such is the political state to which all the tribes and families of the Vril-ya seek to attain, and towards that goal all our theories of government are shaped. You see how utterly opposed is such a progress to that of the uncivilised nations from which you come, and which aim at a systematic perpetuity of troubles, and cares, and warring passions, aggravated more and more as their progress storms its way onward. The most powerful of all the races in our world, beyond the pale of the Vril-ya, esteems itself the best governed of all political societies, and to have reached in that respect the extreme end at which political wisdom can arrive, so that the other nations should tend more or less to copy it. It has established, on its broadest base, the *Koom-Posh*﻿—viz., the government of the ignorant upon the principle of being the most numerous. It has placed the supreme bliss in the vying with each other in all things, so that the evil passions are never in repose﻿—vying for power, for wealth, for eminence of some kind; and in this rivalry it is horrible to hear the vituperation, the slanders, and calumnies which even the best and mildest among them heap on each other without remorse or shame.”

“Some years ago,” said Aph-Lin, “I visited this people, and their misery and degradation were the more appalling because they were always boasting of their felicity and grandeur as compared with the rest of their species. And there is no hope that this people, which evidently resembles your own, can improve, because all their notions tend to further deterioration. They desire to enlarge their dominion more and more, in direct antagonism to the truth that, beyond a very limited range, it is impossible to secure to a community the happiness which belongs to a well-ordered family; and the more they mature a system by which a few individuals are heated and swollen to a size above the standard slenderness of the millions, the more they chuckle and exact, and cry out, ‘See by what great exceptions to the common littleness of our race we prove the magnificent results of our system!’ ”

“In fact,” resumed Zee, “if the wisdom of human life be to approximate to the serene equality of immortals, there can be no more direct flying off into the opposite direction than a system which aims at carrying to the utmost the inequalities and turbulences of mortals. Nor do I see how, by any forms of religious belief, mortals, so acting, could fit themselves even to appreciate the joys of immortals to which they still expect to be transferred by the mere act of dying. On the contrary, minds accustomed to place happiness in things so much the reverse of godlike, would find the happiness of gods exceedingly dull, and would long to get back to a world in which they could quarrel with each other.”

===Chinese===
阿弗-林和琪时常私下同我交谈那个上界的政治与社会状况；对于那个上界，琪以如此哲学的确信，假定其居民迟早要因弗里尔-雅人的降临而被灭绝。他们在我的叙述中——我在其中继续竭尽所能（又不至堕入太过坐实的谎话，免得被听者的精明轻易识破）把我们和我们自身的力量摆到最讨好的角度——不断找到比较的材料，把我们最文明的人口同那些较低下的地下种族相比；后者在他们看来，是无可救药地沉沦于野蛮之中，纵然必归于灭绝，也只是缓慢地灭绝。但两人在一点上意见一致：他们不愿让他们的社区过早开启通向阳光照临之地的一切入口；两人都心怀仁慈，一念及消灭数以百万计的生灵便不寒而栗；而我为我们生活所画的图景，尽管着色已浓，仍使他们悲伤。我徒然夸耀我们的伟人——诗人、哲学家、演说家、将领——并向弗里尔-雅人挑战，要他们举出堪与匹敌者。“唉！”琪说道，她那庄严的脸柔和成一种天使般的怜悯，“这种少数人对多数人的宰制，正是一个无可救药地野蛮的种族最确凿、最致命的标志。你难道看不出：凡人幸福的首要条件，就在于消灭个人之间的那种争斗与竞争么？无论他们采用何种政体，那争斗与竞争总使多数从属于少数，摧毁个人的真实自由——不管国家的名义自由如何——并取消存在的那种平静，而没有那种平静，身心的至福便无从获得？我们的观念是：人生愈能同化为那样一种存在——我们最高贵的观念所能设想坟墓彼岸精灵的存在——那么，我们在此世便愈接近一种神圣的幸福，也愈容易滑入来生的种种境况。因为，凡我们对诸神或蒙福不朽者之生活所能想象的，必定没有自造的忧虑和好斗的情欲，诸如贪婪与野心。在我们看来，那必定是一种宁静安然的生活——并非没有智识或灵性能力的积极活动，但那些活动无论性质如何，须与各人的禀性相契，而非强迫与厌恶——一种因温柔情谊的自在交流而欢畅的生活，其中道德的氛围彻底杀灭仇恨与报复、争斗与竞胜。弗里尔-雅人的一切部落与家族所力求达到的，正是这样的政治状态；我们的一切统治理论，都是朝着那个目标塑成的。你看得出，这样的进步与你所来自的那些未开化民族的进步何其相反——后者以烦恼、忧惧与交战的情欲之系统地永续为鹄的，且其进步愈是奔腾向前，这一切便愈演愈烈。我们世界中弗里尔-雅人范围以外的一切种族里最强大的那一族，自认为是一切政治社会中治理最佳的，认为在这方面已达到政治智慧所能抵达的极致，故而其他各国都应或多或少起而仿效。它以最广的基础确立了库姆-波什（Koom-Posh）——即以人最众多为原则的无知者之治。它把至高的幸福安放在凡事彼此争胜之中，于是种种恶念永无宁时——争权、争财、争某种名位；而在这争胜之中，连他们中间最好最温和的人也毫无悔疚羞耻地互相堆加谩骂、诽谤与中伤，听来实在骇人。”

“数年之前，”阿弗-林说，“我访问过这个民族；他们的悲惨与堕落之所以愈发骇人，正因为他们总是自夸其幸福与宏大，说非其同类可比。而这个显然同你所属民族相像的民族，是无望改良的，因为他们的一切观念都趋向进一步的败坏。他们渴望把统治之地扩张得愈来愈广，直接违背了这条真理：超出一个极有限的幅度，便不可能使一个社会获得属于一个良序家庭的幸福；他们愈是使其制度成熟——借这制度，少数个人被烘热、胀大到远逾千百万人标准瘦削的体量——他们便愈是沾沾自得、索取无厌，并且大声喊道：‘看哪，凭我们种族普遍渺小之中的这些何等伟大的例外，证明了我们制度辉煌的成果！’”

“事实上，”琪接着说，“倘若人生的智慧在于接近不朽者那种宁静的平等，那么，凡以求把凡人的不平等与骚乱推到极致为宗旨的制度，便是朝着相反方向最直接的飞离。我也看不出，凭着任何形式的宗教信仰，如此行事的凡人怎能把自己训练得哪怕仅仅领略不朽者的种种快乐——他们还指望着仅凭一死便被迁到那些快乐中去呢。相反，惯于把幸福安放在如此迥异于神性之事上面的心智，会觉得诸神的幸福乏味之至，会渴望回到一个可以彼此争吵的世界里去。”

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
I have spoken so much of the *Vril* Staff that my reader may expect me to describe it. This I cannot do accurately, for I was never allowed to handle it for fear of some terrible accident occasioned by my ignorance of its use; and I have no doubt that it requires much skill and practice in the exercise of its various powers. It is hollow, and has in the handle several stops, keys, or springs by which its force can be altered, modified, or directed﻿—so that by one process it destroys, by another it heals﻿—by one it can rend the rock, by another disperse the vapour﻿—by one it affects bodies, by another it can exercise a certain influence over minds. It is usually carried in the convenient size of a walking-staff, but it has slides by which it can be lengthened or shortened at will. When used for special purposes, the upper part rests in the hollow of the palm with the fore and middle fingers protruded. I was assured, however, that its power was not equal in all, but proportioned to the amount of certain *vril* properties in the wearer in affinity, or rapport with the purposes to be effected. Some were more potent to destroy, others to heal, etc.; much also depended on the calm and steadiness of volition in the manipulator. They assert that the full exercise of *vril* power can only be acquired by constitutional temperament﻿—i.e., by hereditarily transmitted organisation﻿—and that a female infant of four years old belonging to the Vril-ya races can accomplish feats with the wand placed for the first time in her hand, which a life spent in its practice would not enable the strongest and most skilled mechanician, born out of the pale of the Vril-ya, to achieve. All these wands are not equally complicated; those entrusted to children are much simpler than those borne by sages of either sex, and constructed with a view to the special object in which the children are employed; which, as I have before said, is among the youngest children the most destructive. In the wands of wives and mothers the correlative destroying force is usually abstracted, the healing power fully charged. I wish I could say more in detail of this singular conductor of the *vril* fluid, but its machinery is as exquisite as its effects are marvellous.

I should say, however, that this people have invented certain tubes by which the *vril* fluid can be conducted towards the object it is meant to destroy, throughout a distance almost indefinite; at least I put it modestly when I say from 500 to 600 miles. And their mathematical science as applied to such purpose is so nicely accurate, that on the report of some observer in an air-boat, any member of the *vril* department can estimate unerringly the nature of intervening obstacles, the height to which the projectile instrument should be raised, and the extent to which it should be charged, so as to reduce to ashes within a space of time too short for me to venture to specify it, a capital twice as vast as London.

===Chinese===
我已如此多地谈到弗里尔杖（the *Vril* Staff），读者或许指望我对它作一番描述。这一点我无法做得精确，因为我从未获准握持它，唯恐我因不知其用法而酿成某种可怕的事故；而且我毫不怀疑，运用它的种种力量需要大量的技巧与练习。它是空心的，柄上有若干音栓、键或弹簧，凭借它们，它的力量可以改变、调节或导引——于是以一种作用它毁灭，以另一种它治疗——以一种它能劈裂岩石，以另一种它能驱散蒸气——以一种它作用于形体，以另一种它能对心智施加一定的影响。它通常以手杖那样的便利尺寸携持，但装有滑套，可以随意放长缩短。供特殊用途时，其上部托于掌心凹处，食指与中指伸出。然而人们向我保证，它的力量在人各不同，而是与执杖者体内某些弗里尔（vril）禀性之多寡成比例——那些禀性与所要达成的目的相亲和、相感通。有些人更长于毁灭，另一些人更长于治疗，等等；这在很大程度上还取决于操作者意志的平静与坚定。他们断言，弗里尔之力的充分运用，唯有凭禀赋的气质方能获得——即凭遗传传递的机体——并说弗里尔-雅诸族中一个四岁的女婴，初次把杖拿到手中便能完成的技艺，一个出生在弗里尔-雅范围之外的最强壮、最熟练的技工，纵然毕生练习，也无从企及。这些杖并非同样复杂；交托给儿童的比两性贤哲所执的要简单得多，并且是照儿童所从事的特定目标构造的——如我先前所说，那目标在最年幼的儿童中间是最具毁灭性的。在妻子与母亲的杖中，相应的毁灭之力通常被抽去，治疗之力则充注盈满。但愿我能更详细地谈谈这件弗里尔流体的奇特导体，然而它的机械之精妙，一如其功效之神奇。

不过我应当说，这个民族发明了某些管筒，能把弗里尔流体导向它所要毁除的目标，贯穿几乎无限的距离；至少，当我说 500 至 600 英里时，我已说得极为含蓄了。他们应用于此类目的的数学科学又如此精确，以至于凭飞舟中某个观察者的报告，弗里尔部门的任何一个成员都能无误地估定中途障碍的性质、投射器械应升至的高度以及应充注的程度，从而在一段短得令我不敢贸然指明的时间内，把一座两倍于伦敦的巨城化为灰烬。

===Original===
Certainly these Ana are wonderful mathematicians﻿—wonderful for the adaptation of the inventive faculty to practical uses.

I went with my host and his daughter Zee over the great public museum, which occupies a wing in the College of Sages, and in which are hoarded, as curious specimens of the ignorant and blundering experiments of ancient times, many contrivances on which we pride ourselves as recent achievements. In one department, carelessly thrown aside as obsolete lumber, are tubes for destroying life by metallic balls and an inflammable powder, on the principle of our cannons and catapults, and even still more murderous than our latest improvements.

My host spoke of these with a smile of contempt, such as an artillery officer might bestow on the bows and arrows of the Chinese. In another department there were models of vehicles and vessels worked by steam, and of an air-balloon which might have been constructed by Montgolfier. “Such,” said Zee, with an air of meditative wisdom﻿—“such were the feeble triflings with nature of our savage forefathers, ere they had even a glimmering perception of the properties of *vril*!”

===Chinese===
诚然，这些安-纳人（the Ana）是了不起的数学家——了不起在于把发明的才能调适于实际的用途。

我随主人及其女儿琪（Zee）一同参观了宏大的公共博物馆，它占着贤哲学院（College of Sages）的一翼；其中囤积着许多机巧之物，被当作古代无知而笨拙的实验的珍奇标本，而我们对这些机巧之物正自诩为晚近的成就。在一个部门里，凭金属弹丸和一种易燃之粉来毁灭生命的管筒，被随随便便地丢在一旁，当作过时的废物——其原理与我们的火炮和投石机相同，甚至比我们最新的改良还要凶残。

主人带着轻蔑的微笑谈到这些玩意儿，就像一位炮兵军官对中国的弓箭可能给予的那种微笑。另一个部门里陈列着以蒸汽驱动的车辆与船只的模型，还有一只或许出自蒙戈尔菲耶（Montgolfier）之手的气球。“这等玩意儿，”琪带着沉思的智慧之神情说道，“便是我们野蛮的祖先对自然的微弱戏弄，那时他们连弗里尔种种性质的微光都还没有窥见呢！”

===Original===
This young *Gy* was a magnificent specimen of the muscular force to which the females of her country attain. Her features were beautiful, like those of all her race: never in the upper world have I seen a face so grand and so faultless, but her devotion to the severer studies had given to her countenance an expression of abstract thought which rendered it somewhat stern when in repose; and such a sternness became formidable when observed in connection with her ample shoulders and lofty stature. She was tall even for a *Gy*, and I saw her lift up a cannon as easily as I could lift a pocket-pistol. Zee inspired me with a profound terror﻿—a terror which increased when we came into a department of the museum appropriated to models of contrivances worked by the agency of *vril*; for here, merely by a certain play of her *vril* staff, she herself standing at a distance, she put into movement large and weighty substances. She seemed to endow them with intelligence, and to make them comprehend and obey her command. She set complicated pieces of machinery into movement, arrested the movement or continued it, until, within an incredibly short time, various kinds of raw material were reproduced as symmetrical works of art, complete and perfect. Whatever effect mesmerism or electro-biology produces over the nerves and muscles of animated objects, this young *Gy* produced by the motions of her slender rod over the springs and wheels of lifeless mechanism.

When I mentioned to my companions my astonishment at this influence over inanimate matter﻿—while owning that, in our world, I had witnessed phenomena which showed that over certain living organisations certain other living organisations could establish an influence genuine in itself, but often exaggerated by credulity or craft﻿—Zee, who was more interested in such subjects than her father, bade me stretch forth my hand, and then, placing it beside her own, she called my attention to certain distinctions of type and character. In the first place, the thumb of the *Gy* (and, as I afterwards noticed, of all that race, male or female) was much larger, at once longer and more massive, than is found with our species above ground. There is almost, in this, as great a difference as there is between the thumb of a man and that of a gorilla. Secondly, the palm is proportionally thicker than ours﻿—the texture of the skin infinitely finer and softer﻿—its average warmth is greater. More remarkable than all this, is a visible nerve, perceptible under the skin, which starts from the wrist skirting the ball of the thumb, and branching, fork-like, at the roots of the fore and middle fingers. “With your slight formation of thumb,” said the philosophical young *Gy*, “and with the absence of the nerve which you find more or less developed in the hands of our race, you can never achieve other than imperfect and feeble power over the agency of *vril*; but so far as the nerve is concerned, that is not found in the hands of our earliest progenitors, nor in those of the ruder tribes without the pale of the Vril-ya. It has been slowly developed in the course of generations, commencing in the early achievements, and increasing with the continuous exercise, of the *vril* power; therefore, in the course of one or two thousand years, such a nerve may possibly be engendered in those higher beings of your race, who devote themselves to that paramount science through which is attained command over all the subtler forces of nature permeated by *vril*. But when you talk of matter as something in itself inert and motionless, your parents or tutors surely cannot have left you so ignorant as not to know that no form of matter is motionless and inert: every particle is constantly in motion and constantly acted upon by agencies, of which heat is the most apparent and rapid, but *vril* the most subtle, and, when skilfully wielded, the most powerful. So that, in fact, the current launched by my hand and guided by my will does but render quicker and more potent the action which is eternally at work upon every particle of matter, however inert and stubborn it may seem. If a heap of metal be not capable of originating a thought of its own, yet, through its internal susceptibility to movement, it obtains the power to receive the thought of the intellectual agent at work on it; by which, when conveyed with a sufficient force of the *vril* power, it is as much compelled to obey as if it were displaced by a visible bodily force. It is animated for the time being by the soul thus infused into it, so that one may almost say that it lives and reasons. Without this we could not make our automata supply the place of servants.”

I was too much in awe of the thews and the learning of the young *Gy* to hazard the risk of arguing with her. I had read somewhere in my schoolboy days that a wise man, disputing with a Roman Emperor, suddenly drew in his horns; and when the emperor asked him whether he had nothing further to say on his side of the question, replied, “Nay, Caesar, there is no arguing against a reasoner who commands ten legions.”

===Chinese===
这位年轻的姬（Gy），是其国中女子所能达到的筋肉力量的一个出色标本。她容貌美丽，一如她全族之人：我在上界从不曾见过如此庄严、如此完美无瑕的面容；但她对那些更严苛学业的潜心，使她面容带上了一种抽象思考的表情，静默时便显得有几分冷峻；而这冷峻，一经联系到她宽阔的肩膀与高挑的身量来看，便变得令人生畏了。即使以姬的标准而论，她也算高，我亲眼见她举起一门炮，轻松得就像我举起一支袖珍手枪。琪使我深感恐怖——当我们走进博物馆中专陈凭弗里尔作用驱动的种种机巧模型的那个部门时，这恐怖更有增无已；因为在这里，仅仅凭她弗里尔杖的某种挥运，她本人远远站着，便使巨大沉重的物体运动起来。她仿佛赋予它们以智性，使它们懂得并服从她的命令。她使复杂的机械运转起来，又使运转停止或继续，直到在短得难以置信的时间内，各种各样的原料被再造为匀称的艺术品，完整而完美。无论催眠术或电生物学在活物的神经与肌肉上产生何种效果，这位年轻的姬都凭她那细杖在无生命机械的弹簧与轮齿上的挥动产生了出来。

当我向同伴们说出我对这种支配无生命物质之力的惊异时——我一面承认，在我们的世界里，我曾目睹一些现象，表明某些活着的机体确能对另一些活着的机体建立一种本身真实的影响，只是常被轻信或狡诈所夸大——琪对这些题目比她父亲更感兴趣，便叫我伸出手来，把它放在她自己的手旁，请我注意类型与构造上的某些差别。首先，姬的拇指——以及我后来注意到的，那个种族无论男女的拇指——比我们地上人类的要大得多，既更长且更厚重；其中的差异，几乎同人的拇指与大猩猩的拇指之间的差异一般大。其次，手掌比我们的相应更厚——皮肤的肌理无限地更细更软——其平均的温度也更高。比这一切更可注意的，是一条肉眼可见的神经，在皮下隐约可辨，它从腕部发出，沿拇指球侧行，到食指与中指的根部便叉状分岔。“凭你们这般纤细的拇指构造，”这位好哲思的年轻姬说道，“又缺少那根在我们种族手中或多或少发达的神经，你们对弗里尔之作用的支配，便永远只能是不完全而微弱的；但就那根神经而言，它也不见于我们最早祖先的手中，更不见于弗里尔-雅范围以外较粗野部落的手中。它是许多世代间慢慢发展出来的，始于弗里尔之力早年的成就，随其不断的运用而增长；因此，在一两千年间，这样的神经或许也能在你们种族那些较高的存在者身上孕育出来——只要他们献身于那门至高的科学，借以获得对弗里尔所渗透的自然一切精微之力的支配。至于你说物质本身是惰滞不动的东西，你的父母或师长远不至于把你教得无知到不知道：没有任何形态的物质是静止而惰性的：每一微粒都在不断运动，不断受种种作用的影响，其中热最显见而迅疾，弗里尔则最精微，而一经巧妙运用，也最强大。所以，事实上，由我的手发出、由我的意志导引的这股流，只不过是使那永恒作用于每一物质微粒的作用——无论它看来多么惰滞顽梗——变得更快更有力罢了。一堆金属纵然不能自己生出一个念头，然而凭着它内部对运动的感受性，它便获得了接受那作用于它的智性施动者之念的能力；这念头一旦带着足够的弗里尔之力传来，它便不得不服从，仿佛被一种可见的身体之力移开一般。有那么一刻，它被如此注入其中的灵魂所活化，以至于人们几乎可以说它是活着且能推理的。没有这个，我们的自动机器便不能代替仆役了。”

我对这位年轻姬的筋力与学问实在敬畏太过，不敢冒同她争辩的风险。我在学生时代读到过：一位智者同罗马皇帝论辩，忽然敛锋收势；皇帝问他，在他这一方是否再没有什么可说的了，他答道：“没有了，凯撒，同一位统率十个军团的论辩者是辩不倒的。”

===Original===
Though I had a secret persuasion that, whatever the real effects of *vril* upon matter, Mr. Faraday could have proved her a very shallow philosopher as to its extent or its causes, I had no doubt that Zee could have brained all the Fellows of the Royal Society, one after the other, with a blow of her fist. Every sensible man knows that it is useless to argue with any ordinary female upon matters he comprehends; but to argue with a *Gy* seven feet high upon the mysteries of *vril*﻿—as well argue in a desert, and with a simoon!

Amid the various departments to which the vast building of the College of Sages was appropriated, that which interested me most was devoted to the archaeology of the Vril-ya, and comprised a very ancient collection of portraits. In these the pigments and groundwork employed were of so durable a nature that even pictures said to be executed at dates as remote as those in the earliest annals of the Chinese, retained much freshness of colour. In examining this collection, two things especially struck me:﻿—1st, That the pictures said to be between 6,000 and 7,000 years old were of a much higher degree of art than any produced within the last 3,000 or 4,000 years; and, 2nd, That the portraits within the former period much more resembled our own upper world and European types of countenance. Some of them, indeed, reminded me of the Italian heads which look out from the canvas of Titian﻿—speaking of ambition or craft, of care or of grief, with furrows in which the passions have passed with iron ploughshare. These were the countenances of men who had lived in struggle and conflict before the discovery of the latent forces of *vril* had changed the character of society﻿—men who had fought with each other for power or fame as we in the upper world fight.

The type of face began to evince a marked change about a thousand years after the *vril* revolution, becoming then, with each generation, more serene, and in that serenity more terribly distinct from the faces of labouring and sinful men; while in proportion as the beauty and the grandeur of the countenance itself became more fully developed, the art of the painter became more tame and monotonous.

===Chinese===
尽管我私下确信，无论弗里尔对物质的真正效果如何，法拉第先生（Mr. Faraday）本可以证明，就其范围或成因而论，她实在是一位十分浅薄的哲学家；但我也毫不怀疑，琪能凭她的一拳，把皇家学会的全体会员一个接一个打得脑浆迸裂。凡明白事理的人都知道，同任何寻常女子争论他所通晓的事情是无用的；而同一位七英尺高的姬争论弗里尔的奥秘——那还不如到沙漠里去，同一场西蒙风（simoon）辩论呢！

在贤哲学院那座宏大建筑分拨给各部门的种种馆区之中，最使我感兴趣的是专陈弗里尔-雅人古物的那个部门，其中收有一批年代极其古老的画像。这些画像所用的颜料与底子都极其耐久，以致连那些据说绘于同中国最早史册一样遥远的年代的画，也还保留着相当鲜活的色泽。检视这批收藏时，有两点尤其令我震动：第一，那些据说已有 6,000 至 7,000 年之久的画，其艺术造诣远高于近 3,000 或 4,000 年间产生的任何作品；第二，前一时期的那些画像，远更近似于我们上界和欧洲人的容貌类型。其中一些确实使我想起从提香（Titian）画布上望出来的那些意大利人的头颅——诉说着野心或狡诈、忧劳或悲戚，面孔上的沟壑，是种种情欲以铁犁犁过留下的。这是些生活在争斗与冲突之中的人的面容，其时弗里尔的潜在力量尚未被发现、尚未改变社会的性质——是为权力或名望而彼此相斗的人，一如我们在上界的争斗。

约莫在弗里尔革命一千年之后，面容的类型开始显出显著的变化，自此每经一代便更趋宁静，而在那宁静之中，便愈加可畏地区别于劳苦而有罪之人的面孔；与此同时，面容本身的美与庄严愈充分地发展，画家的艺术便愈变得平淡而单调。

===Original===
But the greatest curiosity in the collection was that of three portraits belonging to the prehistorical age, and, according to mythical tradition, taken by the orders of a philosopher, whose origin and attributes were as much mixed up with symbolical fable as those of an Indian Budh or a Greek Prometheus.

From this mysterious personage, at once a sage and a hero, all the principal sections of the Vril-ya race pretend to trace a common origin.

The portraits are of the philosopher himself, of his grandfather, and great-grandfather. They are all at full length. The philosopher is attired in a long tunic which seems to form a loose suit of scaly armour, borrowed, perhaps, from some fish or reptile, but the feet and hands are exposed: the digits in both are wonderfully long, and webbed. He has little or no perceptible throat, and a low receding forehead, not at all the ideal of a sage’s. He has bright brown prominent eyes, a very wide mouth and high cheekbones, and a muddy complexion. According to tradition, this philosopher had lived to a patriarchal age, extending over many centuries, and he remembered distinctly in middle life his grandfather as surviving, and in childhood his great-grandfather; the portrait of the first he had taken, or caused to be taken, while yet alive﻿—that of the latter was taken from his effigies in mummy. The portrait of the grandfather had the features and aspect of the philosopher, only much more exaggerated: he was not dressed, and the colour of his body was singular; the breast and stomach yellow, the shoulders and legs of a dull bronze hue: the great-grandfather was a magnificent specimen of the Batrachian genus, a Giant Frog, *pur et simple*.

===Chinese===
但收藏中最奇特的，是三幅属于史前时代的画像；据神话传说，它们是奉一位哲人之命绘制的——此人的来历与属性，同印度的佛陀或希腊的普罗米修斯的一样，大都同象征性的寓言纠缠在一起。

弗里尔-雅种族的一切主要支系，都自称源出于这位神秘人物——他既是贤者，又是英雄。

这些画像画的是哲人本人、他的祖父和曾祖父，全是全身像。哲人身着一件长袍，那长袍仿佛构成一副宽松的鳞甲，兴许是从某种鱼或爬虫借取来的；但手足裸露：手足的指都出奇地长，且带蹼。他几乎没有看得见的脖颈，额部低平后倾，全然不是贤者的理想型。他生着明亮的褐色暴眼，一张很宽的嘴和高高的颧骨，面色泥浊。据传说，这位哲人活到了族长般的高寿，绵延许多世纪；他中年时还清楚地记得祖父尚在人间，孩提时记得曾祖父尚在人间。前者的画像是他趁其尚在世时绘下、或命人绘下的——后者的画像则取自其木乃伊遗像。祖父的画像具有哲人的容貌与神态，只是夸张得多：他不著衣物，身体颜色奇特；胸腹黄色，肩与腿呈暗青铜色：至于曾祖父，则是两栖类中一个堂堂的标本——一只巨蛙，不折不扣（*pur et simple*）的巨蛙。

===Original===
Among the pithy sayings which, according to tradition, the philosopher bequeathed to posterity in rhythmical form and sententious brevity, this is notably recorded: “Humble yourselves, my descendants; the father of your race was a *twat* (tadpole): exalt yourselves, my descendants, for it was the same Divine Thought which created your father that develops itself in exalting you.”

Aph-Lin told me this fable while I gazed on the three Batrachian portraits. I said in reply: “You make a jest of my supposed ignorance and credulity as an uneducated *Tish*, but though these horrible daubs may be of great antiquity, and were intended, perhaps, for some rude caricature, I presume that none of your race, even in the less enlightened ages, ever believed that the great-grandson of a Frog became a sententious philosopher; or that any section, I will not say of the lofty Vril-ya, but of the meanest varieties of the human race, had its origin in a Tadpole.”

“Pardon me,” answered Aph-Lin: “in what we call the Wrangling or Philosophical Period of History, which was at its height about seven thousand years ago, there was a very distinguished naturalist, who proved to the satisfaction of numerous disciples such analogical and anatomical agreements in structure between an *An* and a Frog, as to show that out of the one must have developed the other. They had some diseases in common; they were both subject to the same parasitical worms in the intestines; and, strange to say, the *An* has, in his structure, a swimming-bladder, no longer of any use to him, but which is a rudiment that clearly proves his descent from a Frog. Nor is there any argument against this theory to be found in the relative difference of size, for there are still existent in our world Frogs of a size and stature not inferior to our own, and many thousand years ago they appear to have been still larger.”

===Chinese===
据传说，哲人以韵文的形式、格言式的简赅，给后世留下了许多精辟的话，其中有一句格外著称：“谦卑吧，我的后裔；你们种族的父亲原是一只蝌蚪（*twat*，tadpole）：自尊吧，我的后裔，因为正是那创造了你们父亲的同一神思，在抬高你们之中发展着它自身。”

我凝视那三幅两栖类画像时，阿弗-林（Aph-Lin）对我讲了这个寓言。我回答说：“你们是在拿我这个未受教育的蒂什（Tish）想象中的无知与轻信打趣；不过，这些骇人的涂鸦尽管或许年代久远、或许是意在作某种粗陋的戏画，我想你们种族中谁也不曾相信——即便在较不开明的时代也不曾——青蛙的曾孙会变成一位好发格言的哲学家；也不曾相信任何一个支系——且不说高贵的弗里尔-雅人的支系，就说人类最低微的变种的支系吧——竟起源于一只蝌蚪。”

“恕我直言，”阿弗-林答道，“在我们所谓的史学的争辩期、即哲学期——其鼎盛约在七千年前——有一位极有声望的博物学家，向众多弟子满意地证明了安（An）与蛙在构造上有如此之类的比的与解剖学的一致，足以表明二者之一必由另一发展而来。他们共有某些疾病；两者肠中都同样寄生着同样的寄生虫；而且说来奇怪，安的身体构造里有一个鳔，于他已毫无用处，却是清楚地证明他源出于蛙的一个退化痕迹。至于体型大小的相对差异，也提不出任何反对这个理论的论证，因为在我们这个世界中，至今还存在体量与身形不逊于我们自己的蛙，而数千年前，它们似乎还要更大。”

===Original===
“I understand that,” said I, “because Frogs this enormous are, according to our eminent geologists, who perhaps saw them in dreams, said to have been distinguished inhabitants of the upper world before the Deluge; and such Frogs are exactly the creatures likely to have flourished in the lakes and morasses of your subterranean regions. But pray, proceed.”

“In the Wrangling Period of History, whatever one sage asserted another sage was sure to contradict. In fact, it was a maxim in that age, that the human reason could only be sustained aloft by being tossed to and fro in the perpetual motion of contradiction; and therefore another sect of philosophers maintained the doctrine that the *An* was not the descendant of the Frog, but that the Frog was clearly the improved development of the *An*. The shape of the Frog, taken generally, was much more symmetrical than that of the *An*; beside the beautiful conformation of its lower limbs, its flanks and shoulders, the majority of the Ana in that day were almost deformed, and certainly ill-shaped. Again, the Frog had the power to live alike on land and in water﻿—a mighty privilege, partaking of a spiritual essence denied to the *An*, since the disuse of his swimming-bladder clearly proves his degeneration from a higher development of species. Again, the earlier races of the Ana seem to have been covered with hair, and, even to a comparatively recent date, hirsute bushes deformed the very faces of our ancestors, spreading wild over their cheeks and chins, as similar bushes, my poor *Tish*, spread wild over yours. But the object of the higher races of the Ana through countless generations has been to erase all vestige of connection with hairy vertebrata, and they have gradually eliminated that debasing capillary excrement by the law of sexual selection; the *Gy-ei* naturally preferring youth or the beauty of smooth faces. But the degree of the Frog in the scale of the vertebrata is shown in this, that he has no hair at all, not even on his head. He was born to that hairless perfection which the most beautiful of the Ana, despite the culture of incalculable ages, have not yet attained. The wonderful complication and delicacy of a Frog’s nervous system and arterial circulation were shown by this school to be more susceptible of enjoyment than our inferior, or at least simpler, physical frame allows us to be. The examination of a Frog’s hand, if I may use that expression, accounted for its keener susceptibility to love, and to social life in general. In fact, gregarious and amatory as are the Ana, Frogs are still more so. In short, these two schools raged against each other; one asserting the *An* to be the perfected type of the Frog; the other that the Frog was the highest development of the *An*. The moralists were divided in opinion with the naturalists, but the bulk of them sided with the Frog-preference school. They said, with much plausibility, that in moral conduct (viz., in the adherence to rules best adapted to the health and welfare of the individual and the community) there could be no doubt of the vast superiority of the Frog. All history showed the wholesale immorality of the human race, the complete disregard, even by the most renowned amongst them, of the laws which they acknowledged to be essential to their own and the general happiness and well-being. But the severest critic of the Frog race could not detect in their manners a single aberration from the moral law tacitly recognised by themselves. And what, after all, can be the profit of civilisation if superiority in moral conduct be not the aim for which it strives, and the test by which its progress should be judged?

===Chinese===
“这个我明白，”我说，“因为据我们杰出的地质学家们说——他们或许是在梦里见过——这般巨大的蛙，曾是大洪水之前上界的著名居民；而这样的蛙，恰恰就是最可能在你们地下地域的湖泊与沼泽中繁盛起来的生物。不过请讲下去。”

“在史学的争辩期，一位贤者无论断言什么，另一位贤者必定反驳。事实上，那是那个时代的格言：人类的理性唯有被抛掷于矛盾的不息运动中颠来簸去，才能维持于高处；于是另一派哲学家主张这样的学说：安并非蛙的后裔，倒是蛙显然是安的改进的发展。蛙的形体，总体而论，比安匀称得多；且不说它下肢、两胁与肩部的优美构造，那个时代的安-纳多数几乎可算畸形，而且肯定是形貌不端的。再者，蛙有水陆两栖的能力——一种宏大的特权，带有一种安所不得沾溉的灵性本质，因为他的鳔之废弃清楚地证明他从一个更高的发展退化而来。再者，安-纳的较早各族似乎遍体生毛，甚至直到比较晚近的时代，多毛的丛薮还损毁着我们祖先的脸面，在他们的两颊与下颏上蔓生，正如相类的丛薮，我可怜的蒂什，在你的脸上蔓生一样。但许多世代以来，安-纳较高等各族的目标，就是抹去与有毛脊椎动物的一切关联痕迹，他们已凭性择的法则逐渐汰除了那可鄙的毛发排泄物；姬-埃（the Gy-ei）天性更爱青春，或爱无髭的光洁面孔。而蛙在脊椎动物阶梯上的品位，由此可见：它压根没有毛，连头上也没有。它生来就具有那种无毛的完美，那是安-纳中最美的，尽管修养了不可胜数的世代，也还没有达到的。蛙的神经系统与动脉循环的精妙繁复，被这一学派证明为比我们这副较低下、或至少较简单的体质所容许的更易感受愉悦。对蛙的手的检视——请容我用这个说法——解释了它对爱、对一般社会生活的更敏锐的感受性。事实上，安-纳固然群居而多爱，蛙却更是如此。简而言之，这两派彼此怒争：一派断言安是蛙的完成型；另一派断言蛙是安的最高发展。道德家们与博物学家意见分歧，但大多数站在偏爱蛙的一派。他们说得颇为有理：在道德品行上（即遵守最合乎个人与社会之健康与福祉的规则上），蛙的巨大优越是无可置疑的。全部历史都表明人类大规模的不道德，表明连他们中间最负盛名的人也全然无视那些他们自己承认对其自身及普遍幸福安康必不可少的法则。但对蛙族最苛刻的批评者，也挑不出它们的行为有任何一处越出它们默然公认的道德法则。归根结底，倘若道德品行上的优越既不是文明所力求的鹄的，又不是判断其进步的试金石，那么文明究竟还有什么益处呢？”

===Original===
“In fine, the adherents of this theory presumed that in some remote period the Frog race had been the improved development of the Human; but that, from some causes which defied rational conjecture, they had not maintained their original position in the scale of nature; while the Ana, though of inferior organisation, had, by dint less of their virtues than their vices, such as ferocity and cunning, gradually acquired ascendancy, much as among the human race itself tribes utterly barbarous have, by superiority in similar vices, utterly destroyed or reduced into insignificance tribes originally excelling them in mental gifts and culture. Unhappily these disputes became involved with the religious notions of that age; and as society was then administered under the government of the *Koom-Posh*, who, being the most ignorant, were of course the most inflammable class﻿—the multitude took the whole question out of the hands of the philosophers; political chiefs saw that the Frog dispute, so taken up by the populace, could become a most valuable instrument of their ambition; and for not less than one thousand years war and massacre prevailed, during which period the philosophers on both sides were butchered, and the government of *Koom-Posh* itself was happily brought to an end by the ascendancy of a family that clearly established its descent from the aboriginal tadpole, and furnished despotic rulers to the various nations of the Ana. These despots finally disappeared, at least from our communities, as the discovery of *vril* led to the tranquil institutions under which flourish all the races of the Vril-ya.”

“And do no wranglers or philosophers now exist to revive the dispute; or do they all recognise the origin of your race in the tadpole?”

===Chinese===
“总之，这一理论的信徒们推测，在某个遥远的时期，蛙族曾是人类的改进的发展；但由于某些无从合理猜度的原因，它们未能保住自己在自然阶梯上的原位；而安-纳虽然机体较低，却凭他们的恶德——诸如凶猛与狡诈——而非美德，逐渐取得了优势，很像人类自身之中全然野蛮的部落，凭相类恶德上的优越，把智识天赋与文化上原本胜过他们的部落彻底消灭或贬为无足轻重。不幸的是，这些争辩同那个时代的宗教观念纠缠在一起；而其时社会正是在库姆-波什（Koom-Posh）的治理之下施政的——库姆-波什既是最无知者，当然也是最易燃的阶层——民众把整个问题从哲学家手里夺了过去；政治首领们看出，被民众如此拾起的蛙之争，可以成为他们野心的极宝贵工具；于是不少于一千年间，战争与屠戮横行，其间两派的哲学家惨遭屠戮，而库姆-波什的治理本身，也幸而因一个家族的得势而告终——这个家族清楚地确立了自己源出于原始蝌蚪的血统，并为安-纳诸国提供了专制的统治者。这些专制者最终消失了，至少从我们的社区中消失了，因为弗里尔的发现引出了那使弗里尔-雅一切种族得以昌盛的安宁制度。”

“那么现在就没有争辩者或哲学家来重提这场争辩了么？还是他们都承认你们种族源出于蝌蚪？”

===Original===
“Nay, such disputes,” said Zee, with a lofty smile, “belong to the *Pah-bodh* of the dark ages, and now only serve for the amusement of infants. When we know the elements out of which our bodies are composed, elements in common to the humblest vegetable plants, can it signify whether the All-Wise combined those elements out of one form more than another, in order to create that in which He has placed the capacity to receive the idea of Himself, and all the varied grandeurs of intellect to which that idea gives birth? The *An* in reality commenced to exist as *An* with the donation of that capacity, and, with that capacity, the sense to acknowledge that, however through the countless ages his race may improve in wisdom, it can never combine the elements at its command into the form of a tadpole.”

“You speak well, Zee,” said Aph-Lin; “and it is enough for us shortlived mortals to feel a reasonable assurance that whether the origin of the *An* was a tadpole or not, he is no more likely to become a tadpole again than the institutions of the Vril-ya are likely to relapse into the heaving quagmire and certain strife-rot of a *Koom-Posh*.”

===Chinese===
“不，这类争辩，”琪带着高傲的微笑说，“属于黑暗时代的帕-博德（Pah-bodh），如今只配给婴孩作消遣了。当我们知道构成我们身体的那些元素，不过与最卑微的草木所共有的元素相同时，那全智者究竟从这一形态还是那一形态合成这些元素，又有什么要紧呢？——他合成它们，是为了创造那样一种存在，在其中他安放了领受他自身之观念的能力，以及那观念所生出的智识的一切多样的庄严。安实际上是从领受那能力之时，才开始作为安存在的；而随那能力一同来的，还有这样的意识：纵使他的种族历经无数世代在智慧上精进，它也永远不能把它掌握的元素合成一只蝌蚪的形态。”

“你说得好，琪，”阿弗-林说，“对我们这些短寿的凡人来说，能有这样一个合理的把握也就够了：无论安是否起源于蝌蚪，他再变回蝌蚪的可能性，同弗里尔-雅人的制度重新堕入库姆-波什那翻腾的泥沼与必定的争斗腐朽中去的可能性，是一样小的。”

## XVII / 十七

===Original===
The Vril-ya, being excluded from all sight of the heavenly bodies, and having no other difference between night and day than that which they deem it convenient to make for themselves﻿—do not, of course, arrive at their divisions of time by the same process that we do; but I found it easy, by the aid of my watch, which I luckily had about me, to compute their time with great nicety. I reserve for a future work on the science and literature of the Vril-ya, should I live to complete it, all details as to the manner in which they arrive at their rotation of time; and content myself here with saying, that in point of duration, their year differs very slightly from ours, but that the divisions of their year are by no means the same. Their day (including what we call night) consists of twenty hours of our time, instead of twenty-four, and of course their year comprises the correspondent increase in the number of days by which it is summed up. They subdivide the twenty hours of their day thus﻿—eight hours,4 called the “Silent Hours,” for repose; eight hours, called the “Earnest Time,” for the pursuits and occupations of life; and four hours called the “Easy Time” (with which what I may term their day closes), allotted to festivities, sport, recreation, or family converse, according to their several tastes and inclinations. But, in truth, out of doors there is no night. They maintain, both in the streets and in the surrounding country, to the limits of their territory, the same degree of light at all hours. Only, within doors, they lower it to a soft twilight during the Silent Hours. They have a great horror of perfect darkness, and their lights are never wholly extinguished. On occasions of festivity they continue the duration of full light, but equally keep note of the distinction between night and day, by mechanical contrivances which answer the purpose of our clocks and watches. They are very fond of music; and it is by music that these chronometers strike the principal division of time. At every one of their hours, during their day, the sounds coming from all the timepieces in their public buildings, and caught up, as it were, by those of houses or hamlets scattered amidst the landscapes without the city, have an effect singularly sweet, and yet singularly solemn. But during the Silent Hours these sounds are so subdued as to be only faintly heard by a waking ear. They have no change of seasons, and, at least on the territory of this tribe, the atmosphere seemed to me very equable, warm as that of an Italian summer, and humid rather than dry; in the forenoon usually very still, but at times invaded by strong blasts from the rocks that made the borders of their domain. But time is the same to them for sowing or reaping as in the Golden Isles of the ancient poets. At the same moment you see the younger plants in blade or bud, the older in ear or fruit. All fruit-bearing plants, however, after fruitage, either shed or change the colour of their leaves. But that which interested me most in reckoning up their divisions of time was the ascertainment of the average duration of life amongst them. I found on minute inquiry that this very considerably exceeded the term allotted to us on the upper earth. What seventy years are to us, one hundred years are to them. Nor is this the only advantage they have over us in longevity, for as few among us attain to the age of seventy, so, on the contrary, few among them die before the age of one hundred; and they enjoy a general degree of health and vigour which makes life itself a blessing even to the last. Various causes contribute to this result: the absence of all alcoholic stimulants; temperance in food; more especially, perhaps, a serenity of mind undisturbed by anxious occupations and eager passions. They are not tormented by our avarice or our ambition; they appear perfectly indifferent even to the desire of fame; they are capable of great affection, but their love shows itself in a tender and cheerful complaisance, and, while forming their happiness, seems rarely, if ever, to constitute their woe. As the *Gy* is sure only to marry where she herself fixes her choice, and as here, not less than above ground, it is the female on whom the happiness of home depends; so the *Gy*, having chosen the mate she prefers to all others, is lenient to his faults, consults his humours, and does her best to secure his attachment. The death of a beloved one is of course with them, as with us, a cause for sorrow; but not only is death with them so much more rare before that age in which it becomes a release, but when it does occur the survivor takes much more consolation than, I am afraid, the generality of us do, in the certainty of reunion in another and yet happier life.

All these causes, then, concur to their healthful and enjoyable longevity, though, no doubt, much also must be owing to hereditary organisation. According to their records, however, in those earlier stages of their society when they lived in communities resembling ours, agitated by fierce competition, their lives were considerably shorter, and their maladies more numerous and grave. They themselves say that the duration of life, too, has increased, and is still on the increase, since their discovery of the invigorating and medicinal properties of *vril*, applied for remedial purposes. They have few professional and regular practitioners of medicine, and these are chiefly *Gy-ei*, who, especially if widowed and childless, find great delight in the healing art, and even undertake surgical operations in those cases required by accident, or, more rarely, by disease.

===Chinese===
弗里尔-雅人（the Vril-ya）既全然见不到天体，昼夜之间又除他们认为方便而自行设定的那种区别外别无差异，当然便不循我们那样的过程来得出他们的时间划分；但我发觉，凭我幸而随身带着的怀表之助，要极其精确地推算他们的时间并不难。至于他们如何达成其时间的轮转，一切细节我都留给一部论弗里尔-雅人科学与文学的将来之作——倘若我能活着完成它的话；在这里只消说：就长短而论，他们的年同我们的相差极微，但其年的划分却绝非相同。他们的“日”（包括我们所谓的夜）由我们时间的二十小时组成，而非二十四小时；于是他们的年自然也相应地在组成它的日数上有所增加。他们把一日二十小时作如下划分——八小时〔4〕，称为“静默时辰”，用于休憩；八小时称为“正经时辰”，用于人生的追求与事务；还有四小时称为“安逸时辰”（我所谓的他们的“日”便以此收束），按各人的趣味与性情，拨给庆宴、游戏、消遣或家常闲谈。但说实话，户外是没有夜的。在街道上，在四郊的乡野间，直到他们疆域的边界为止，他们在一切时辰都保持同等的光明。只是到了静默时辰，他们才在户内把光调低成一派柔和的薄暮。他们对全然的黑暗怀有极大的恐怖，他们的灯从不完全熄灭。逢庆宴之期，他们便延续满光之时长，但同样借种种机械装置记下昼夜的区别——那些装置起着我们钟表的作用。他们酷爱音乐；而这些计时器便是以音乐来报出时间的主要划分的。他们的“日”里每到一个钟点，公共建筑中所有计时器发出的乐声，仿佛又被城外散布于山水之间的宅舍与村落的计时器接续扬起，其效果异常甜美，又异常庄严。但在静默时辰，这些声音便调得极低，唯有醒着的耳朵才能隐约听见。他们没有季节的更替；至少在这个部族的疆土上，气候在我看来极其匀和，温暖如意大利的夏天，湿润而非干燥；午前通常十分沉静，但时而也有从构成他们疆界的高岩袭来的劲风。至于时间，对他们来说播种与收割一如在古代诗人的金岛之上：同一时刻，你会看见较幼的植株正在抽叶或含苞，较老的已经结实或成穗。不过，一切结果的植物，结果之后或落叶，或改换叶色。但在盘点他们的时间划分时，最使我感兴趣的，还是查明他们中间寿命的平均长度。我经细密询问后发现，这远远超过上界分配给我们的年限。我们的七十岁于我们是什么，他们的一百岁于他们便是什么。而这还不是他们在长寿上胜过我们的唯一好处，因为正如我们中间很少有人活到七十岁，相反，他们中间很少有人不满一百岁便死去；而且他们普遍享有那样的健康与精力，使生命本身直到最后一刻仍是一种福分。种种原因共同促成了这一结果：全然没有酒精的刺激；饮食有节；而尤其重要的，或许是心境的安宁，不为焦灼的事务与炽烈的情欲所扰。他们不受我们的贪婪或野心的折磨；他们对名声的欲望似乎也全然淡漠；他们能够深挚地相爱，但他们的爱显为一种温存而愉快的体贴，在构成他们的幸福的同时，几乎从不——即便有过——构成他们的悲苦。既然姬（Gy）必定只在她自己选定之处嫁人，既然在这里——不亚于在地上——家庭的幸福系于女性一身，那么这位姬，既已选中她最爱于一切人的伴侣，便宽容他的过失，体察他的心绪，竭力巩固他的依恋。挚爱者的死亡，在他们那里当然也如在我们这里一样，是悲伤的缘由；但在死亡成为解脱的那个年龄之前，他们的死亡要罕见得多；而死亡一旦降临，生者又从另一种更幸福生活中必将重聚的确信里，得到远比——恕我直言——我们大多数人所得到的更多的慰藉。

这一切原因便共同促成了他们康健而愉快的长寿，尽管无疑也有许多须归功于遗传的机体。然而据他们的记载，在其社会的较早阶段——那时他们生活在同我们相仿的、为剧烈竞争所激荡的社区里——他们的寿命要短得多，疾病也更繁多而沉重。他们自己也说，自从发现弗里尔（vril）振奋与药用的性质并将其用于疗治以来，寿命也已经增长，且仍在增长。他们很少有职业的正规行医者，而这些行医者主要是姬-埃（the Gy-ei）；她们——尤其是丧偶无子的——在医术中找到极大的乐趣，甚至在意外事故所需的场合、或在疾病所需但较为罕见的场合，施行外科手术。

===Original===
They have their diversions and entertainments, and, during the Easy Time of their day, they are wont to assemble in great numbers for those winged sports in the air which I have already described. They have also public halls for music, and even theatres, at which are performed pieces that appeared to me somewhat to resemble the plays of the Chinese﻿—dramas that are thrown back into distant times for their events and personages, in which all classic unities are outrageously violated, and the hero, in one scene a child, in the next is an old man, and so forth. These plays are of very ancient composition, and their stories cast in remote times. They appeared to me very dull, on the whole, but were relieved by startling mechanical contrivances, and a kind of farcical broad humour, and detached passages of great vigour and power expressed in language highly poetical, but somewhat overcharged with metaphor and trope. In fine, they seemed to me very much what the plays of Shakespeare seemed to a Parisian in the time of Louis XV, or perhaps to an Englishman in the reign of Charles II.

The audience, of which the *Gy-ei* constituted the chief portion, appeared to enjoy greatly the representation of these dramas, which, for so sedate and majestic a race of females, surprised me, till I observed that all the performers were under the age of adolescence, and conjectured truly that the mothers and sisters came to please their children and brothers.

I have said that these dramas are of great antiquity. No new plays, indeed no imaginative works sufficiently important to survive their immediate day, appear to have been composed for several generations. In fact, though there is no lack of new publications, and they have even what may be called newspapers, these are chiefly devoted to mechanical science, reports of new inventions, announcements respecting various details of business﻿—in short, to practical matters. Sometimes a child writes a little tale of adventure, or a young *Gy* vents her amorous hopes or fears in a poem; but these effusions are of very little merit, and are seldom read except by children and maiden *Gy-ei*. The most interesting works of a purely literary character are those of explorations and travels into other regions of this nether world, which are generally written by young emigrants, and are read with great avidity by the relations and friends they have left behind.

===Chinese===
他们自有其消遣与娱乐；在一天的安逸时辰里，他们惯于大批聚集，作我已描述过的那种空中飞翼游戏。他们还有公共音乐厅，甚至有剧院，里面上演的剧目，在我看来颇似中国人的戏剧——情节与人物都被抛回遥远的时代，一切古典的三一律都被放肆地践踏，主人公上一场还是孩童，下一场已是老人，诸如此类。这些剧本作成极为古老，故事也安放在邈远之世。总体看来它们使我觉着十分沉闷，却因惊人的机械装置、一种滑稽的阔略幽默，以及用高度诗化的语言写出的若干神完气足、笔力遒劲的散段而得到调剂——只是那语言未免隐喻与藻饰过重。总之，它们在我看来，颇像莎士比亚的戏剧在路易十五时代一个巴黎人眼中的样子，或许也像在查理二世治下一个英国人眼中的样子。

观众中姬-埃占了主要的部分，她们看这些戏时似乎兴味极浓；这一族女性如此端庄威严，这颇使我诧异，直到我察觉所有演员都未到青春期，才真切地猜到，母亲们和姐妹们是来让她们的孩子和兄弟高兴的。

我已说过，这些剧本极为古老。好几个世代以来，似乎再没有新的剧本、也再没有重要到足以存留于当世之外的想象作品被创作出来。事实上，新书固然层出不穷，他们甚至有可以称为报纸的东西，但那些主要致力于机械科学、新发明的报道、各项商业细目的通告——总之，都是实务之事。有时一个孩子写一篇小小的冒险故事，或一位年轻的姬在诗中吐露她爱情的希望或忧惧；但这些抒发之作甚少可取，除了孩子和未婚的姬-埃，也很少有人读。纯粹文学性质的作品中最有趣的，是那些探测这个下界其他地域的探险与游记，它们多出于年轻移民之手，被他们留在身后的亲族朋友如饥似渴地读着。

===Original===
I could not help expressing to Aph-Lin my surprise that a community in which mechanical science had made so marvellous a progress, and in which intellectual civilisation had exhibited itself in realising those objects for the happiness of the people, which the political philosophers above ground had, after ages of struggle, pretty generally agreed to consider unattainable visions, should, nevertheless, be so wholly without a contemporaneous literature, despite the excellence to which culture had brought a language at once so rich and simple, vigorous and musical.

My host replied﻿—“Do you not perceive that a literature such as you mean would be wholly incompatible with that perfection of social or political felicity at which you do us the honour to think we have arrived? We have at last, after centuries of struggle, settled into a form of government with which we are content, and in which, as we allow no differences of rank, and no honours are paid to administrators distinguishing them from others, there is no stimulus given to individual ambition. No one would read works advocating theories that involved any political or social change, and therefore no one writes them. If now and then an *An* feels himself dissatisfied with our tranquil mode of life, he does not attack it; he goes away. Thus all that part of literature (and to judge by the ancient books in our public libraries, it was once a very large part) which relates to speculative theories on society is become utterly extinct. Again, formerly there was a vast deal written respecting the attributes and essence of the All-Good, and the arguments for and against a future state; but now we all recognise two facts, that there *is* a Divine Being, and there *is* a future state, and we all equally agree that if we wrote our fingers to the bone, we could not throw any light upon the nature and conditions of that future state, or quicken our apprehensions of the attributes and essence of that Divine Being. Thus another part of literature has become also extinct, happily for our race; for in the times when so much was written on subjects which no one could determine, people seemed to live in a perpetual state of quarrel and contention. So, too, a vast part of our ancient literature consists of historical records of wars and revolutions during the times when the Ana lived in large and turbulent societies, each seeking aggrandisement at the expense of the other. You see our serene mode of life now; such it has been for ages. We have no events to chronicle. What more of us can be said than that ‘they were born, they were happy, they died?’ Coming next to that part of literature which is more under the control of the imagination, such as what we call Glaubsila, or colloquially ‘Glaubs,’ and you call poetry, the reasons for its decline amongst us are abundantly obvious.

===Chinese===
我不禁向阿弗-林（Aph-Lin）说出我的惊异：一个机械科学已取得如此惊人进步的社区，一个其智识文明已在实现那些目标——即上界的政治哲学家们历经世代斗争后已大致公认为不可企及之幻梦的、为人民幸福而设的目标——上显露自身的社区，竟全然没有与之并世的文学，尽管教养已把一种既丰富又简洁、既刚健又铿锵的语言带到了极致。

我的主人答道——“你难道看不出，你所说的那种文学，会同你认为——承你的抬举——我们已经达到的那种社会或政治之幸福的圆满全然不能相容么？经过数世纪的斗争，我们终于安定了下来，落定为一种我们满意的政体；在这种政体里，我们既不容等级的差异，也不给行政者任何把他们区别于他人的荣衔，于是个人的野心便无从受激。谁也不会去读鼓吹任何政治或社会变革之理论的书，因此也没有谁去写。倘若偶尔有一个安（An）对我们的恬静生活感到不满，他并不攻击它；他一走了之。于是，文学中一切关涉社会思辨理论的部分（而据我们公共图书馆里的古书判断，它一度是很大的一部分）已全然绝迹。再者，从前关于至善者（the All-Good）的属性与本质、关于来世之有无的论证，著述浩繁；但如今我们都承认两个事实：确有一位神明存在，确有一个来世存在；我们又一致同意，哪怕我们写到指骨尽露，也不能对那个来世的性质与境况投射一丝光亮，也不能加深我们对那位神明的属性与本质的领会。于是文学的另一部分也绝迹了，这是我们种族的幸事；因为在那个众人对无人能断定之事大发议论的时代，人们仿佛生活在无休止的口角与纷争之中。同样，我们古代文学的很大一部分，是安-纳（the Ana）生活在庞大而骚乱的社会中时那些战争与革命的历史记载——那时各邦皆以损人来自肥。你看到我们如今这种恬静的生活；千百年来一直如此。我们没有什么大事可载。关于我们，除了‘他们生了，他们幸福过，他们死了’，还能多说什么呢？再说到文学中更多受想象支配的那部分，比如我们所谓格劳布西拉（Glaubsila）——口语叫‘格劳布斯’（Glaubs）——而你们称为诗的东西，它在我们中间衰微的原因，是再明显不过的了。”

===Original===
“We find, by referring to the great masterpieces in that department of literature which we all still read with pleasure, but of which none would tolerate imitations, that they consist in the portraiture of passions which we no longer experience﻿—ambition, vengeance, unhallowed love, the thirst for warlike renown, and suchlike. The old poets lived in an atmosphere impregnated with these passions, and felt vividly what they expressed glowingly. No one can express such passions now, for no one can feel them, or meet with any sympathy in his readers if he did. Again, the old poetry has a main element in its dissection of those complex mysteries of human character which conduce to abnormal vices and crimes, or lead to signal and extraordinary virtues. But our society, having got rid of temptations to any prominent vices and crimes, has necessarily rendered the moral average so equal, that there are no very salient virtues. Without its ancient food of strong passions, vast crimes, heroic excellences, poetry therefore is, if not actually starved to death, reduced to a very meagre diet. There is still the poetry of description﻿—description of rocks, and trees, and waters, and common household life; and our young *Gy-ei* weave much of this insipid kind of composition into their love verses.”

“Such poetry,” said I, “might surely be made very charming; and we have critics amongst us who consider it a higher kind than that which depicts the crimes, or analyses the passions, of man. At all events, poetry of the insipid kind you mention is a poetry that nowadays commands more readers than any other among the people I have left above ground.”

“Possibly; but then I suppose the writers take great pains with the language they employ, and devote themselves to the culture and polish of words and rhythms of an art?”

===Chinese===
“我们只要检视那一部门文学中的伟大杰作——那些我们至今读来仍觉愉快、却谁也容忍不了对之模仿的作品——便发现它们全在于描绘我们不再经历的种种情欲：野心、复仇、渎乱之爱、对战功声名的渴求，诸如此类。老诗人们生活在饱浸着这些情欲的氛围里，强烈地感受着他们热烈地表达的东西。如今谁也不能表达这样的情欲了，因为谁也感受不到；即便感受得到，在读者那里也引不起共鸣。再者，老诗的一个主要成分，是对人性中那些复杂奥秘的剖析——它们或导向反常的恶德与罪愆，或引向卓越非凡的美德。但我们的社会既已摆脱了一切显著恶德与罪愆的诱惑，便必然使道德的平均水准如此齐一，以至于不再有十分突出的美德。失去了强烈情欲、滔天大罪、英雄品格这古老的食粮，诗于是纵然不曾真正饿死，也只落得一份极其菲薄的口粮。剩下的还有描写之诗——描写岩石、树木、流水与寻常家宅生活的诗；我们的年轻姬-埃便把许多这类寡淡的成分织进她们的情诗里。”

“这样的诗，”我说，“必定可以弄得十分迷人；我们中间就有批评家认为，它比那种描画人的罪愆、剖析人的情欲的诗更高一等。无论如何，你所说的那种寡淡之诗，在我留在上界的同胞中间，如今拥有的读者比任何别的诗都多。”

“也许吧；不过我想，那些作者对他们所用的语言是下过大功夫的，他们潜心于一门艺术的词句与韵律的培育和打磨吧？”

===Original===
“Certainly they do: all great poets do that. Though the gift of poetry may be inborn, the gift requires as much care to make it available as a block of metal does to be made into one of your engines.”

“And doubtless your poets have some incentive to bestow all those pains upon such verbal prettinesses?”

“Well, I presume their instinct of song would make them sing as the bird does; but to cultivate the song into verbal or artificial prettiness, probably does need an inducement from without, and our poets find it in the love of fame﻿—perhaps, now and then, in the want of money.”

===Chinese===
“他们当然如此：一切大诗人都是如此。诗才纵然可能是天生的，但要使它派上用场，同把一块金属制成你们的一种机器一样，都需要下同样的功夫。”

“而且无疑，你们的诗人把所有这些苦功下在这类词藻的妍丽上，总有某种诱因吧？”

“呃，我想他们歌唱的本能会使他们像鸟儿那样歌唱；但要把歌唱培育成词藻的、即人工的妍丽，多半确实需要外来的诱导，而我们的诗人是在对名声的热爱中找到它的——有时呢，则是因为缺钱。”

===Original===
“Precisely so. But in our society we attach fame to nothing which man, in that moment of his duration which is called ‘life,’ can perform. We should soon lose that equality which constitutes the felicitous essence of our commonwealth if we selected any individual for preeminent praise: preeminent praise would confer preeminent power, and the moment it were given, evil passions, now dormant, would awake; other men would immediately covet praise, then would arise envy, and with envy hate, and with hate calumny and persecution. Our history tells us that most of the poets and most of the writers who, in the old time, were favoured with the greatest praise, were also assailed by the greatest vituperation, and even, on the whole, rendered very unhappy, partly by the attacks of jealous rivals, partly by the diseased mental constitution which an acquired sensitiveness to praise and to blame tends to engender. As for the stimulus of want; in the first place, no man in our community knows the goad of poverty; and, secondly, if he did, almost every occupation would be more lucrative than writing.

“Our public libraries contain all the books of the past which time has preserved; those books, for the reasons above stated, are infinitely better than any can write nowadays, and they are open to all to read without cost. We are not such fools as to pay for reading inferior books, when we can read superior books for nothing.”

===Chinese===
“正是如此。但在我们的社会里，凡人在其绵延中被称作‘生命’的那一瞬所能做成的事，我们一概不附以名声。倘若我们选出任何个人给予超群的褒奖，我们便很快会失去那种构成我们共同体幸福本质的平等：超群的褒奖会授予超群的权力，而褒奖一经给出，如今蛰伏的恶念便会醒来；旁人立刻会觊觎褒奖，于是生出嫉妒，随嫉妒而来的便是憎恨，随憎恨而来的便是诽谤与迫害。我们的历史告诉我们：旧时蒙受最高赞誉的大多数诗人和大多数作者，也遭到最厉害的辱骂，而且总的说来落得非常不幸——一部分是由于嫉妒的对手的攻击，一部分是由于对褒贬养成的高度敏感容易造成的那种病态的心性。至于匮乏的刺激：首先，我们社区中没有人尝过贫穷的鞭笞；其次，纵然尝过，几乎任何职业都比写作更有利可图。”

“我们的公共图书馆收藏着时间保存下来的一切过去的书；出于上述原因，那些书远胜于今日任何人所能写出的，而且任人免费阅读。既然好书可以白读，还要花钱去读坏书，我们还没有蠢到这个地步。”

===Original===
“With us, novelty has an attraction; and a new book, if bad, is read when an old book, though good, is neglected.”

“Novelty, to barbarous states of society struggling in despair for something better, has no doubt an attraction, denied to us, who see nothing to gain in novelties; but after all, it is observed by one of our great authors four thousand years ago, that ‘he who studies old books will always find in them something new, and he who reads new books will always find in them something old.’ But to return to the question you have raised, there being then amongst us no stimulus to painstaking labour, whether in desire of fame or in pressure of want, such as have the poetic temperament, no doubt, vent it in song, as you say the bird sings; but for lack of elaborate culture it fails of an audience, and, failing of an audience, dies out, of itself, amidst the ordinary avocations of life.”

===Chinese===
“在我们那里，新奇自有其吸引力；一本新书哪怕坏，也有人读；一本旧书哪怕好，也被冷落。”

“对于在绝望中挣扎着求取更好东西的野蛮社会，新奇无疑有一种吸引力——那是我们所没有的，因为我们看不出新奇之中有何可图；但归根到底，正如我们的一位大作者四千年前所说：‘研读旧书的人总会从中发现新东西，而读新书的人总会从中发现旧东西。’不过，回到你提出的那个问题上来：我们中间既然没有苦心劳作的刺激——无论是对名声的渴望还是匮乏的压迫——那么禀有诗人气质的人，无疑如你所说的鸟儿一般，以歌吟发泄它；但苦于缺乏精心的培育，它得不到听众；而得不到听众，便在人生的寻常营生中自行消亡了。”

===Original===
“But how is it that these discouragements to the cultivation of literature do not operate against that of science?”

“Your question amazes me. The motive to science is the love of truth apart from all consideration of fame, and science with us too is devoted almost solely to practical uses, essential to our social conversation and the comforts of our daily life. No fame is asked by the inventor, and none is given to him; he enjoys an occupation congenial to his tastes, and needing no wear and tear of the passions. Man must have exercise for his mind as well as body; and continuous exercise, rather than violent, is best for both. Our most ingenious cultivators of science are, as a general rule, the longest lived and the most free from disease. Painting is an amusement to many, but the art is not what it was in former times, when the great painters in our various communities vied with each other for the prize of a golden crown, which gave them a social rank equal to that of the kings under whom they lived. You will thus doubtless have observed in our archaeological department how superior in point of art the pictures were several thousand years ago. Perhaps it is because music is, in reality, more allied to science than it is to poetry, that, of all the pleasurable arts, music is that which flourishes the most amongst us. Still, even in music the absence of stimulus in praise or fame has served to prevent any great superiority of one individual over another; and we rather excel in choral music, with the aid of our vast mechanical instruments, in which we make great use of the agency of water,5 than in single performers. We have had scarcely any original composer for some ages. Our favorite airs are very ancient in substance, but have admitted many complicated variations by inferior, though ingenious, musicians.”

===Chinese===
“可这些挫抑文学培育的东西，为什么不对科学的培育起作用呢？”

“你的问题令我诧异。科学的动机是对真理的热爱，全然不掺对名声的考虑；而我们的科学也几乎专用于实际的用途，于我们的社交往来与日常生活的舒适必不可少。发明者不求名声，也没有名声给他；他享受着一门合乎他趣味的营生，无需损耗情志。人的心智同身体一样必须运动；而持续的运动，而非剧烈的运动，于二者都最有益。我们最富巧思的科学耕耘者，一般说来最为长寿，也最少疾病。绘画对许多人是一种消遣，但这门艺术已不复当年——那时我们各个社区的大画家们彼此竞逐金冠之奖，那桂冠给予他们同他们所事的君王相等的社会地位。这样，你在我们的古物部门想必已经看出，几千年前的画在艺术上是何等高超。也许正因为音乐实际上与其说近于诗，不如说近于科学，在一切怡悦之艺中，音乐才是在我们中间最繁荣的。不过，即便在音乐中，褒赞与名声之刺激的缺失，也使任何个人对他人不能有巨大的超越；我们所长于的是合唱之乐，凭我们宏大的机械乐器之助——其中我们大量利用水的作用〔5〕——而非独奏名家。好些世代以来，我们几乎没有原创的作曲家。我们心爱的曲调实质上极为古老，只是经了虽属二流、却富巧思的乐师之手，添入了繁复的变奏。”

===Original===
“Are there no political societies among the Ana which are animated by those passions, subjected to those crimes, and admitting those disparities in condition, in intellect, and in morality, which the state of your tribe, or indeed of the Vril-ya generally, has left behind in its progress to perfection? If so, among such societies perhaps Poetry and her sister arts still continue to be honoured and to improve?”

“There are such societies in remote regions, but we do not admit them within the pale of civilised communities; we scarcely even give them the name of Ana, and certainly not that of Vril-ya. They are savages, living chiefly in that low stage of being, *Koom-Posh*, tending necessarily to its own hideous dissolution in *Glek-Nas*. Their wretched existence is passed in perpetual contest and perpetual change. When they do not fight with their neighbours, they fight among themselves. They are divided into sections, which abuse, plunder, and sometimes murder each other, and on the most frivolous points of difference that would be unintelligible to us if we had not read history, and seen that we too have passed through the same early state of ignorance and barbarism. Any trifle is sufficient to set them together by the ears. They pretend to be all equals, and the more they have struggled to be so, by removing old distinctions, and starting afresh, the more glaring and intolerable the disparity becomes, because nothing in hereditary affections and associations is left to soften the one naked distinction between the many who have nothing and the few who have much. Of course the many hate the few, but without the few they could not live. The many are always assailing the few; sometimes they exterminate the few; but as soon as they have done so, a new few starts out of the many, and is harder to deal with than the old few. For where societies are large, and competition to have something is the predominant fever, there must be always many losers and few gainers. In short, they are savages groping their way in the dark towards some gleam of light, and would demand our commiseration for their infirmities, if, like all savages, they did not provoke their own destruction by their arrogance and cruelty. Can you imagine that creatures of this kind, armed only with such miserable weapons as you may see in our museum of antiquities, clumsy iron tubes charged with saltpetre, have more than once threatened with destruction a tribe of the Vril-ya, which dwells nearest to them, because they say they have thirty millions of population﻿—and that tribe may have fifty thousand﻿—if the latter do not accept their notions of *Soc-Sec* (money getting) on some trading principles which they have the impudence to call ‘a law of civilisation’?”

===Chinese===
“安-纳之中就没有那样一些政治社会，仍为那些情欲所鼓动、仍受那些罪愆的支配、仍容许那些境况、智识与道德上的悬殊么？——你们的部族、乃至弗里尔-雅人整体，在臻于完善的进程中已把这些抛在身后了。倘若有的话，在那样一些社会中，诗和她的姊妹之艺或许还继续受人尊崇、还在精进吧？”

“这样的社会在遥远的地域是有的，但我们不承认它们属于文明社区之列；我们几乎连安-纳之名都不肯给它们，更不必说弗里尔-雅之名了。他们是野蛮人，主要生活在那种低下的生存阶段，即库姆-波什（Koom-Posh），并必然趋向其在格莱克-纳斯（Glek-Nas）中的可怖解体。他们悲惨的日子在无尽的争斗与无尽的变更中度过。不同邻邦交战时，便自相残杀。他们分成许多派系，彼此辱骂、劫掠，有时彼此谋杀，而所争的又是最琐屑的分歧点——若不是我们读过历史、见过我们自己也曾经历同一早期的愚昧与野蛮阶段，那些分歧点对我们来说简直无从索解。任何一点鸡毛蒜皮都足以叫他们扭打起来。他们自命人人平等；而他们愈是靠废除旧的差别、从头来过以求平等，那悬殊便愈发刺眼、愈发不可容忍，因为世袭的情谊与连属已荡然无存，再没有什么来缓和那唯一的赤裸裸的差别——一无所出的多数人与广有资财的少数人之间的差别。当然，多数人恨少数人，但没有少数人他们便活不成。多数人永远在攻打少数人；有时他们把少数人斩尽杀绝；但一旦杀绝，新的少数人立刻又从多数人中蹿出，而且比老的少数人更难对付。因为凡社会庞大、而竞争攫取成为主导的热病之处，必定输家众多而赢家寥寥。总之，他们是些在黑暗中摸索着走向一线微光的野蛮人，倘若他们不像一切野蛮人那样以傲慢与残忍自招毁灭，本可以博得我们对他们的弱点的怜悯。你能想象么：这样的生灵，仅凭着你们可以在我们古物博物馆里见到的那种可怜武器——装填硝石的粗笨铁管——竟不止一次以毁灭相威胁，胁迫离他们最近的一个弗里尔-雅人部族——他们说他们有三千万人口，而那个部族兴许只有五万——如果后者不接受他们那套索克-塞克（Soc-Sec，即牟财之术）的观念，接受他们某些无耻地称作‘文明之法’的通商原则的话？”

===Original===
“But thirty millions of population are formidable odds against fifty thousand!”

My host stared at me astonished. “Stranger,” said he, “you could not have heard me say that this threatened tribe belongs to the Vril-ya; and it only waits for these savages to declare war, in order to commission some half-a-dozen small children to sweep away their whole population.”

At these words I felt a thrill of horror, recognising much more affinity with “the savages” than I did with the Vril-ya, and remembering all I had said in praise of the glorious American institutions, which Aph-Lin stigmatised as *Koom-Posh*. Recovering my self-possession, I asked if there were modes of transit by which I could safely visit this temerarious and remote people.

===Chinese===
“可是三千万人口对五万，这众寡之势也未免太悬殊了吧！”

我的主人愕然瞪着我。“外乡人，”他说，“你准是没有听清我说这个受威胁的部族属于弗里尔-雅人；它只等这些野蛮人宣战，好委派六七个小孩去把他们整个人口一扫而光。”

听了这话，我感到一阵恐怖的震栗：我觉察自己同“野蛮人”的亲缘，远过于同弗里尔-雅人的亲缘；又想起我曾对那光荣的美国制度说过的所有赞语——那制度，阿弗-林曾讥之为库姆-波什。我定了定神，便问可有交通的途径，让我得以安然探访这个鲁莽而遥远的民族。

===Original===
“You can travel with safety, by *vril* agency, either along the ground or amid the air, throughout all the range of the communities with which we are allied and akin; but I cannot vouch for your safety in barbarous nations governed by different laws from ours; nations, indeed, so benighted, that there are among them large numbers who actually live by stealing from each other, and one could not with safety in the Silent Hours even leave the doors of one’s own house open.”

Here our conversation was interrupted by the entrance of Taë, who came to inform us that he, having been deputed to discover and destroy the enormous reptile which I had seen on my first arrival, had been on the watch for it ever since his visit to me, and had began to suspect that my eyes had deceived me, or that the creature had made its way through the cavities within the rocks to the wild regions in which dwelt its kindred race﻿—when it gave evidences of its whereabouts by a great devastation of the herbage bordering one of the lakes. “And,” said Taë, “I feel sure that within that lake it is now hiding. So,” (turning to me) “I thought it might amuse you to accompany me to see the way we destroy such unpleasant visitors.” As I looked at the face of the young child, and called to mind the enormous size of the creature he proposed to exterminate, I felt myself shudder with fear for him, and perhaps fear for myself, if I accompanied him in such a chase. But my curiosity to witness the destructive effects of the boasted *vril*, and my unwillingness to lower myself in the eyes of an infant by betraying apprehensions of personal safety, prevailed over my first impulse. Accordingly, I thanked Taë for his courteous consideration for my amusement, and professed my willingness to set out with him on so diverting an enterprise.

===Chinese===
“凭弗里尔之力的驱动，无论沿地面还是在空中，你可以安然游历我们所有联盟同宗的社区的整个范围；但在那些受着与我们不同的法律治理的野蛮国度里，我不能为你的安全作保；那些国家实在蒙昧得很，其中竟有大群人真的靠互相偷窃为生，静默时辰里，连自己家的大门都不敢敞着。”

谈话至此，塔伊（Taë）进来打断了我们；他来告诉我们：他既受委派去寻出并消灭我初到时所见到的那条巨爬虫，自那次来访我之后便一直守候着它，并且已开始疑心是我的眼睛骗了我，或那生物已从岩石内部的洞穴逃到它的同族所居的蛮荒地域去了——不料它以湖滨草地的一场大破坏，显明了它的所在。“而且，”塔伊说，“我确信它此刻就藏在那湖里。所以，”（转向我）“我想，随我同去看看我们消灭这类讨厌来客的办法，或许可以给你解个闷。”我望着这年轻孩子的脸，想起他要猎杀的那生物的庞大身躯，不禁为他打了个寒颤——倘若我陪他去追猎，或许也为自己害怕。但是，我既渴欲亲眼见证那备受夸耀的弗里尔的毁灭性效果，又不愿在一个婴孩眼前露出对自身安危的忧惧而自贬身份，这两者便压倒了我的第一冲动。于是，我谢过塔伊对我消遣的殷勤体谅，并表示乐意随他同赴这样一场有趣的猎奇。

## XVIII / 十八

===Original===
As Taë and myself, on quitting the town, and leaving to the left the main road which led to it, struck into the fields, the strange and solemn beauty of the landscape, lighted up, by numberless lamps, to the verge of the horizon, fascinated my eyes, and rendered me for some time an inattentive listener to the talk of my companion.

Along our way various operations of agriculture were being carried on by machinery, the forms of which were new to me, and for the most part very graceful; for among these people art being so cultivated for the sake of mere utility, exhibits itself in adorning or refining the shapes of useful objects. Precious metals and gems are so profuse among them, that they are lavished on things devoted to purposes the most commonplace; and their love of utility leads them to beautify its tools, and quickens their imagination in a way unknown to themselves.

In all service, whether in or out of doors, they make great use of automaton figures, which are so ingenious, and so pliant to the operations of *vril*, that they actually seem gifted with reason. It was scarcely possible to distinguish the figures I beheld, apparently guiding or superintending the rapid movements of vast engines, from human forms endowed with thought.

===Chinese===
当塔伊（Taë）与我离开城郭，把通往城中的大道撇在左手、折入田野之际，那一派景色——被无数明灯一直照亮到天际的尽头——其奇特而庄严之美令我双目迷醉，以致有一阵子，我竟成了同伴谈话时漫不经心的听者。

沿途可见各种农事作业正由机器进行，其形制于我皆属初见，且大多十分优美；因为在这个民族中间，艺术既纯然为实用之故而加以培植，便显露于对有用之物的形状的装点与完善之中。贵金属与宝石在他们中间如此丰沛，以致被挥霍于用途最寻常之物上；他们对实用的热爱，引领他们去美化实用的工具，并以一种他们自己也不自知的方式，激发着他们的想象力。

凡一切仆役之务，无论户内户外，他们都大量使用自动人偶；这些人偶构造如此精巧，对弗里尔（vril）的作用又如此顺从灵便，看上去简直像被赋予了理性。我眼中所见的那些人影，仿佛正在引导或监督庞大机器的迅疾运转，要把它们同被赋予思想的人形区别开来，几乎是不可能的事。

===Original===
By degrees, as we continued to walk on, my attention became roused by the lively and acute remarks of my companion. The intelligence of the children among this race is marvellously precocious, perhaps from the habit of having entrusted to them, at so early an age, the toils and responsibilities of middle age. Indeed, in conversing with Taë, I felt as if talking with some superior and observant man of my own years. I asked him if he could form any estimate of the number of communities into which the race of the Vril-ya is subdivided.

“Not exactly,” he said, “because they multiply, of course, every year as the surplus of each community is drafted off. But I heard my father say that, according to the last report, there were a million and a half of communities speaking our language, and adopting our institutions and forms of life and government; but, I believe, with some differences, about which you had better ask Zee. She knows more than most of the *Ana* do. An *An* cares less for things that do not concern him than a *Gy* does; the *Gy-ei* are inquisitive creatures.”

===Chinese===
随着我们继续前行，同伴那些活泼而锐利的议论渐渐唤起了我的注意。这个民族的儿童，智力早熟得惊人，这或许是因为他们习惯于在如此幼小的年纪，便把中年人的辛劳与责任托付到他们身上。的确，与塔伊交谈时，我觉得自己仿佛在同一位与我年纪相若、却更高明而善于观察的人说话。我问他，能否估计一下弗里尔-雅人（the Vril-ya）这一种族分成了多少个社群。

“说不确切，”他说，“因为每个社群的多余人口年年分流出去，数目当然逐年增多。不过我听父亲说，据最近一次的报告，讲我们的语言、采用我们的制度与生活及治理形式的，共有一百五十万个社群；只是我相信其间略有差异，这一点你最好去问琪（Zee）。她比大多数安-纳（Ana）知道的都多。一个安（An）对与他无关之事的关心，比一个姬（Gy）要少；姬-埃（Gy-ei）是好奇的生灵。”

===Original===
“Does each community restrict itself to the same number of families or amount of population that you do?”

“No; some have much smaller populations, some have larger﻿—varying according to the extent of the country they appropriate, or to the degree of excellence to which they have brought their machinery. Each community sets its own limit according to circumstances, taking care always that there shall never arise any class of poor by the pressure of population upon the productive powers of the domain; and that no state shall be too large for a government resembling that of a single well-ordered family. I imagine that no *vril* community exceeds thirty thousand households. But, as a general rule, the smaller the community, provided there be hands enough to do justice to the capacities of the territory it occupies, the richer each individual is, and the larger the sum contributed to the general treasury﻿—above all, the happier and the more tranquil is the whole political body, and the more perfect the products of its industry. The state which all tribes of the Vril-ya acknowledge to be the highest in civilisation, and which has brought the *vril* force to its fullest development, is perhaps the smallest. It limits itself to four thousand families; but every inch of its territory is cultivated to the utmost perfection of garden ground; its machinery excels that of every other tribe, and there is no product of its industry in any department which is not sought for, at extraordinary prices, by each community of our race. All our tribes make this state their model, considering that we should reach the highest state of civilisation allowed to mortals if we could unite the greatest degree of happiness with the highest degree of intellectual achievement; and it is clear that the smaller the society the less difficult that will be. Ours is too large for it.”

===Chinese===
“每个社群都把自家的户数或人口限制得和你们一样吗？”

“不；有的社群人口少得多，有的则更多——或依其占用国土的广狭而异，或依其机器所臻完善的程度而异。每个社群都因地制宜自定限度，唯须始终留意：绝不容人口压迫疆域的生产力而滋生出任何贫民阶层；也绝不容任何国家庞大得不再适于一个如同井然有序的单一家庭般的政府。我想，没有一个弗里尔社群超过三万户。但大体而论，只要人手足以尽用其所占疆土的产能，社群越小，各人便越富，输往公共金库的款项也越多——而首要的是，整个政治机体越幸福、越安宁，其工业的产品也越完善。被弗里尔-雅人所有部族公认为文明最高、并把弗里尔之力发展到最充分地步的那个国家，或许正是最小的。它把自己限制在四千户；但它每一寸国土都耕作到园圃般的极致完美；它的机器冠于其他一切部族，其工业无论哪一部门的产品，没有一样不是我们种族的每个社群不惜重价搜求的。我们所有的部族都奉这个国家为楷模，认为倘若能把最大程度的幸福与最高程度的智识成就结合起来，我们便达到了凡人所容许的文明的至高境界；而显而易见，社会越小，这一点便越不难做到。我们的国家要达成它，未免太大了。”

===Original===
This reply set me thinking. I reminded myself of that little state of Athens, with only twenty thousand free citizens, and which to this day our mightiest nations regard as the supreme guide and model in all departments of intellect. But then Athens permitted fierce rivalry and perpetual change, and was certainly not happy. Rousing myself from the reverie into which these reflections had plunged me, I brought back our talk to the subjects connected with emigration.
